Discussion Name:
Should women-only spaces be open to anyone identifying as a woman?

1. Thesis:
Women-only spaces should be open to anyone identifying as a woman.

  1.1. Con:
  Trans women and cis women have more differences than commonalities. Therefore, they should not share the same spaces.

    1.1.1. Con:
    Both cis and trans women are victims of daily and systemic sexism. Women-only spaces can provide them a respite from such oppressive forces (p. 6 [1]).

      1.1.1.1. Pro:
      Both trans and cis women are required to perform [2] femininity [3]; society tells them how they should look and act.

        1.1.1.1.1. Con:
        Women are free to disregard and defy others' expectations [4] about whether or how they express their femininity.

        1.1.1.1.2. Pro:
        Both cis and trans women who fail to perform femininity in traditional ways are subject to discrimination [5].

      1.1.1.2. Pro:
      The vast majority of both cis and trans [6] women are victims of sexual harassment [7], and many are victims of sexual assault [8].

        1.1.1.2.1. Pro:
        A women-only music festival in Sweden was established to protect trans and cis women and nonbinary people from the rampant sexual abuse [9] perpetrated by men at festivals.

        1.1.1.2.2. Con:
        Men also experience sexual harassment [10], but they are not granted access to the support available in women-only spaces.

          1.1.1.2.2.1. Pro:
          Trans men have noted [11] that their sexual harassment claims were taken more seriously before they transitioned.

            1.1.1.2.2.1.1. Pro:
            Trans men experience a high rate of verbal, physical, and sexual harassment [12].

          1.1.1.2.2.2. Con:
          Protection from sexual harassment is not the single unifying purpose of women-only spaces; if men require support for this issue, they can create their own spaces.

          1.1.1.2.2.3. Con:
          Although men do experience sexual harassment, research has consistently shown that women experience it at a substantially higher rate (pp. 41-42 [13]).

          1.1.1.2.2.4. Pro:
          Men experience sexual harassment at a greater rate than is commonly assumed and historically have greatly underreported the incidence of such experiences (pp. 56-57, 60 [14]).

        1.1.1.2.3. Pro:
        In many parts of the world, women are regularly groped or sexually assaulted on public transportation (p. 2 [15]).

          1.1.1.2.3.1. Pro:
          Many cities [16] around the world have implemented women-only spaces on public transportation [17] vehicles to protect women from harassment or being groped by men (p. 30 [18]).

            1.1.1.2.3.1.1. Con:
            Most cities that have implemented women-only metro carriages have not experienced a decrease in the rate of sexual assault [19].

            1.1.1.2.3.1.2. Con:
            Women-only carriages are impractical and not particularly popular among women (pp. 30-31 [20]).

            1.1.1.2.3.1.3. Con:
            There is little clarity regarding whether trans women and nonbinary people are permitted to ride in the women-only sections of public transportation (p. 30 [21]).

            1.1.1.2.3.1.4. Pro:
            Two-thirds of women in Mexico City said they felt safer using women-only transportation options (p. 94 [22]).

              1.1.1.2.3.1.4.1. Pro:
              Half of those who disagreed said that they would feel safer if there were a better way of preventing men from unlawfully accessing these spaces (p. 94 [23]).

              1.1.1.2.3.1.4.2. Con:
              The overwhelming majority of people in Mexico City do not believe that women-only transportation solves any of the public safety issues faced by women (p. 96 [24]).

          1.1.1.2.3.2. Con:
          Women are most commonly assaulted [25] at or near their home, suggesting that public transportation is not as dangerous in comparison.

        1.1.1.2.4. Pro:
        The #MeToo [26] movement consists of stories of harassment and sexual assault from both cis and trans women.

        1.1.1.2.5. Con:
        Women sexually harass and assault [27] other women.

          1.1.1.2.5.1. Pro:
          Some cis lesbian women have experienced pressure and coercion [28] to accept trans women as sexual partners despite their preferences. Allowing trans women into women-only spaces increases their risk of being harmed.

            1.1.1.2.5.1.1. Con:
            Cis lesbians have expressed outrage [29] over the publicity devoted to this issue, claiming that anti-trans activists have attempted to weaponize [30] them against trans people.

            1.1.1.2.5.1.2. Pro:
            Cis lesbian women risk being labeled as transphobic [31] if they refuse to accept trans women as partners.

          1.1.1.2.5.2. Pro:
          Trans women may be particularly susceptible to bullying and harassment by cis women (p. 12 [32]).

          1.1.1.2.5.3. Con:
          While there have been isolated cases of women harassing women, nearly all sexual harassment against women is perpetrated by men (p. 1223 [33]).

            1.1.1.2.5.3.1. Con:
            The public understanding of sexual harassment overlooks some of its common manifestations (p. 2 [34]). There is evidence that women perpetrate gender harassment not motivated by sexual desire [35] at a greater rate than is commonly accepted (pp. 42-43 [36]).

        1.1.1.2.6. Con:
        There is evidence to suggest that the contributions and experiences of trans women in sexual assault recovery groups are not given equal consideration (pp. 54-55 [37]).

      1.1.1.3. Pro:
      Both cis and trans women are systematically denied the advantages and rights that are associated with male privilege [38].

        1.1.1.3.1. Pro:
        Women experience a lack of privilege from a young age, especially if they are also a minority or belong to any other marginalized community (p. 19 [39]).

          1.1.1.3.1.1. Pro:
          Due to socialization, any privileges women experience are the result of benevolent sexism, a patriarchal practice that actually oppresses women (pp. 3-4 [40]).

        1.1.1.3.2. Con:
        Many trans women have experienced male privilege [41] in the past, while cis women have not.

          1.1.1.3.2.1. Con:
          Many trans women do not experience male privilege before and during their transition because of prejudicial attitudes towards their feminine attributes [42].

            1.1.1.3.2.1.1. Pro:
            Gender nonconforming men and boys, some of whom might later come out as trans, are often bullied [43] at school and may face repression from intolerant parents.

            1.1.1.3.2.1.2. Con:
            Male privilege begins at birth; no matter how young someone transitioned or how effeminate they appear or act, they still benefited from being born male.

              1.1.1.3.2.1.2.1. Con:
              The male sex is associated with a higher rate of unfavorable perinatal outcomes [44].

              1.1.1.3.2.1.2.2. Pro:
              In some cultures, the preference for sons over daughters [45] results in sex-selective female abortions, female infanticide, and female neglect from birth onward.

            1.1.1.3.2.1.3. Pro:
            Some individuals come out as trans before they reach puberty, while they are still a child [46].

          1.1.1.3.2.2. Con:
          Some trans women have used their awareness, knowledge, and experience of male privilege to advocate for the rights of all women (p. 334 [47]).

          1.1.1.3.2.3. Pro:
          Even though trans women may no longer benefit from male privilege after transition, they may continue to benefit from having had such privilege in the past.

            1.1.1.3.2.3.1. Pro:
            If a trans woman had been earning a higher wage prior to transitioning, she would have had the opportunity to amass more savings and accrue wealth through investments, asset appreciation, and interest and dividends. This is an unfair advantage over cis women.

            1.1.1.3.2.3.2. Con:
            The experience of male privilege [48] that trans women have pre-transition can be undermined by the fact that they experience oppression through not being able to identify with their true gender at that point in time.

            1.1.1.3.2.3.3. Con:
            Having been privileged in the past does not necessarily have any bearing over current or future states, especially when it comes to transitioning into a historically disenfranchised demographic.

        1.1.1.3.3. Pro:
        Post-transition trans women lose [49] any male privilege they might have had before. The immediate experience of trans women and cis women in these spaces is, therefore, more similar than different.

          1.1.1.3.3.1. Pro:
          Changes in how trans women are treated before and after their transition serve as evidence of male privilege [50], which can be used to advocate for equal rights for women.

        1.1.1.3.4. Con:
        Defining womanhood as a shared experience of oppression is disempowering [51] and promotes a victim mentality.

      1.1.1.4. Con:
      Cis women and trans women suffer because of misogyny in different ways, reducing their ability to relate to one another [52].

        1.1.1.4.1. Pro:
        Cis women do not experience [53] a struggle to be seen as women or get hormones, and they don't have to worry about finding doctors who understand a trans woman’s body.

        1.1.1.4.2. Pro:
        Misogyny forces trans women to attempt an impossible balancing act [54] between being treated as a parody for trying too hard to be feminine and being told they aren't trying hard enough.

        1.1.1.4.3. Con:
        Not all women-only spaces focus on experiences specific to cis women. Such spaces can remain cis-women-only, and others can be open to women not AFAB.

        1.1.1.4.4. Con:
        Experiencing misogyny differently does not imply the need for a separate space. Problems are experienced differently by each individual, but this does not mean that people need to be segregated.

        1.1.1.4.5. Pro:
        It may be difficult for cis and trans women to share their experiences of misogyny, as they may feel they are excluding others in doing so or are being excluded if their experiences don't align with the group.

      1.1.1.5. Con:
      Since trans women are socialized [55] as men from birth until transition, they have internalized misogynistic attitudes and have not been victimized by systemic sexism.

        1.1.1.5.1. Pro:
        Trans women are actively taught [56] the attitudes and behaviors of toxic masculinity [57] as children.

          1.1.1.5.1.1. Con:
          Since trans women do not identify as men, they are less likely to adhere to toxic masculine attitudes than cis men.

            1.1.1.5.1.1.1. Pro:
            Trans women are more likely to be victims [58] than perpetrators of toxic masculinity.

            1.1.1.5.1.1.2. Pro:
            By crafting a personally meaningful gender identity, trans women break gender norms and stereotypes.

            1.1.1.5.1.1.3. Con:
            Trans women are often raised with a sense of male entitlement [59] that can directly contribute to toxic beliefs and behaviors.

          1.1.1.5.1.2. Con:
          Toxic masculinity can affect the attitudes and behaviors of all genders [60].

            1.1.1.5.1.2.1. Pro:
            Since cis women can be also affected by toxic masculinity, trans women do not necessarily have more internalized sexist attitudes than cis women.

          1.1.1.5.1.3. Pro:
          The toxic masculinity that trans women learn as children does not disappear upon transitioning [61].

        1.1.1.5.2. Pro:
        Trans women face different social pressures compared with cis women during their formative years; trans women are not socialized [62] from an early age to live up to society's beauty expectations or conditioned to accept being an object of male fantasy.

          1.1.1.5.2.1. Pro:
          Trans children face more substantial social pressures than cis children; seventy-three percent of trans women [63] experience gender dysphoria by the age of seven.

          1.1.1.5.2.2. Con:
          Even if trans women do not experience certain forms of sexism at a young age, they still experience other forms of systematic sexism.

          1.1.1.5.2.3. Con:
          Regardless of how they were socialized, many trans women become objects of male fantasy, with men festishizing their bodies [64] as adults.

        1.1.1.5.3. Con:
        The assertion that trans women are socialized as boys is reductive; many trans women identify as trans or nonconforming [65] from a young age, and may have been socialized as such.

        1.1.1.5.4. Con:
        Having misogynistic attitudes and being victimized by sexism are not mutually exclusive.

          1.1.1.5.4.1. Pro:
          Misogyny is a part of a patriarchal society and is not perpetuated exclusively by cisgender men [66]. Women also internalize these attitudes, leading to internalized misogyny [67].

      1.1.1.6. Con:
      Transgender women face specific and focused oppression that cisgender women never will, and it can often come from cisgender women. Trans women need their own spaces to escape from such oppressive forces.

        1.1.1.6.1. Pro:
        Gender-critical feminism [68] is a problem for transgender women, as it can often lead to discrimination. Women-only spaces run by gender-critical feminists are not safe for trans women.

          1.1.1.6.1.1. Pro:
          Gender-critical feminists [69] support [70] the opinion that post-operative transgender women are not women.

            1.1.1.6.1.1.1. Pro:
            The cognitive biases [71] of gender-critical feminists contribute to transphobia.

          1.1.1.6.1.2. Con:
          Women-only spaces should ban hostile anti-trans protesters; their existence [72] should not be used as an excuse to exclude trans women.

        1.1.1.6.2. Con:
        Trans women having their own spaces doesn't imply that they should be banned from women-only spaces. Transgender women experience unique struggles [73], but they may also benefit from the resources and support offered by women-only spaces.

          1.1.1.6.2.1. Pro:
          Women-only spaces are likely to be more established and better funded than trans-only spaces (p. 3 [74]).

        1.1.1.6.3. Pro:
        Trans women do not benefit from cisgender privilege (pp. 1-2 [75]).

          1.1.1.6.3.1. Con:
          Excluding trans women from women-only spaces enforces cisgender privilege [76] as it denies them access to needed facilities.

          1.1.1.6.3.2. Pro:
          Nearly one third of trans people in the US live in poverty, which is almost twice the national average [77].

        1.1.1.6.4. Pro:
        Trans women are disproportionately subjected to bullying, harassment, and violence [78].

          1.1.1.6.4.1. Con:
          Even if trans women are subjected to bullying and harassment by cis women in women-only spaces, they will still be safer, since men are responsible for the vast majority of sexual violence [79].

        1.1.1.6.5. Con:
        Following the same logic, excluding women of color from some services if racists object to their presence would be acceptable.

    1.1.2. Con:
    To access women-only spaces, a person should not have to prove their identity based on shared experiences or struggles as that would be impractical.

      1.1.2.1. Pro:
      Since all women, whether trans or cis, have very different experiences of womanhood [80], this is not a valid reason for excluding trans women.

        1.1.2.1.1. Pro:
        Not all cis women share the same struggles (p. 2 [81]), yet they are not excluded from sharing the same spaces. Excluding women who have different life experiences leads to polarization rather than solidarity.

          1.1.2.1.1.1. Con:
          Black women need separate spaces in order to be their authentic selves [82], since their behavior can be and is often misunderstood by white women. The same logic applies for trans women.

          1.1.2.1.1.2. Pro:
          Despite differences in the experiences of women of differing ethnicities, desegregation has benefited women of all races [83] by helping them learn from one another; the inclusion of trans women could do the same as well.

        1.1.2.1.2. Con:
        Trans women have experienced life and gender issues in a way that is profoundly different [84] from cis women. Comparisons may not be useful.

        1.1.2.1.3. Con:
        A woman [85] is defined as an adult female human, while female [86] refers to a biological sex. Therefore, being a transgender [87] woman requires a person to have been born non-female; ergo, trans women are not women.

          1.1.2.1.3.1. Pro:
          Trans women who have not undergone gender-affirming surgery [88] have male genitalia, even if their personality and psychological makeup are feminine.

            1.1.2.1.3.1.1. Con:
            Genitals determine a person's sex [89], not their gender. A person can be a woman but have male genitals; in fact, many trans people do not undergo gender-affirming surgery [90].

          1.1.2.1.3.2. Con:
          The actual meaning of words evolves over time based on usage, and it need not conform to dictionary definitions. Many people now use the word woman to refer to gender rather than sex [91] and have begun petitioning dictionaries [92] to update their entries.

        1.1.2.1.4. Pro:
        If there is no justification for excluding one group in favor of another, nor any fair method of adjudicating which group's needs are more legitimate, then both groups should have access.

        1.1.2.1.5. Pro:
        Since the term "woman" means a number of different things to different groups of people [93], it is unreasonable to exclude some people because they use a different definition.

          1.1.2.1.5.1. Pro:
          Black women, lesbian women, and all other types of women are encompassed by the term "woman" despite having substantially different life experiences [94].

      1.1.2.2. Con:
      Prior hardship is a necessary criterion for entry into spaces that focus on certain issues (e.g., alcoholism, cancer support). There is no reason why a similar requirement cannot be extended to include the issues faced by women.

        1.1.2.2.1. Pro:
        Women-only spaces that are devoted to such issues should rightfully expect that those who access the space have been affected by the same issues and experiences.

        1.1.2.2.2. Con:
        Treating the identity of women as comparable to diseases is profoundly inaccurate, as it wrongly implies that being a woman is a solely negative experience. Some women-only spaces are devoted to exploring and celebrating womanhood.

      1.1.2.3. Pro:
      Trans women will self-select into women-only spaces where their experiences are most closely aligned with those of the cis women involved.

        1.1.2.3.1. Pro:
        Trans women would have no incentive to access women-only spaces that are irrelevant to them.

        1.1.2.3.2. Con:
        The experiences of trans women inherently do not align with those of cis women, and they need not do so in order to be valid (p. 8 [95]).

      1.1.2.4. Con:
      Requiring trans women to demonstrate their identity by sharing the difficulties that they have experienced with womanhood in order to access women-only spaces would be a minor inconvenience.

        1.1.2.4.1. Con:
        Creating different disclosure and reporting policies for different genders is discriminatory.

        1.1.2.4.2. Pro:
        Cis women do not need to demonstrate such identity because their identity as women is confirmed by biology. Trans women, who claim that identity should be sufficient to allow access, should be willing to discuss their experience to demonstrate that they belong in a space.

          1.1.2.4.2.1. Con:
          Such a policy would be unreasonable, highly variable, and likely to result in altercations over interpretation.

          1.1.2.4.2.2. Con:
          Cis women would be unlikely to be able to confirm their gender without undergoing sex verification checks.

          1.1.2.4.2.3. Pro:
          Identity is intimately related to experience, so discussing and confirming such experiences could be a useful way to determine the truth of a claimed identity (p. iii [96]).

        1.1.2.4.3. Con:
        Trans women who have undergone difficult struggles may not feel comfortable discussing them with strangers simply to access everyday facilities.

        1.1.2.4.4. Pro:
        Trans women undergoing gender-affirming medical procedures are generally required to go through a lengthy evaluation of their gender dysphoria (p. 1 [97]). By virtue of completing such evaluations and interventions, they would already be considered to have met this requirement.

    1.1.3. Pro:
    Some trans women do not appear female.

      1.1.3.1. Pro:
      As opposed to trans women, cis women inherently appear female.

        1.1.3.1.1. Con:
        "Passing" as a gatekeeping mechanism is extremely hurtful [98] to trans people.

          1.1.3.1.1.1. Con:
          For trans women who are able to pass for cis women, passing is not as much of an issue [99], as they can avoid altercations [100] that trans women who are unable to pass cannot avert.

          1.1.3.1.1.2. Pro:
          Trans women must spend a great deal of time and resources upholding damaging cisnormative standards [101] in order to pass as cis women.

        1.1.3.1.2. Con:
        The perpetuation of a cisnormative standard of physical appearance is detrimental to all women, as superficial judgments are no longer relevant in today's diverse society (p. 2 [102]).

          1.1.3.1.2.1. Pro:
          Excluding trans women from women-only spaces can lead to discrimination against gender non-conforming and butch [103] women, or cis women with male features [104], who are often mistaken for men or non-passing trans women [105].

        1.1.3.1.3. Pro:
        The concept of "appearing female" is based upon the common physical characteristics [106] associated with the female sex.

      1.1.3.2. Con:
      Gender identity is not dependent on appearance [107]; a person's identity can be completely different from what their appearance conveys.

        1.1.3.2.1. Con:
        Recognizing gender identity as distinct from appearance can be difficult. This could lead to impractical, inappropriate, or unwarranted conversations when accessing women-only spaces.

        1.1.3.2.2. Pro:
        There is a difference [108] between gender identity and gender expression.

      1.1.3.3. Con:
      Many trans women do appear female.

        1.1.3.3.1. Pro:
        People are often unaware of other people's genitalia in most women-only spaces, since seeing someone undressed is uncommon.

          1.1.3.3.1.1. Con:
          Swimming clubs, sports clubs, and dance rooms have common changing areas where nudity is common [109].

            1.1.3.3.1.1.1. Con:
            Men's locker rooms [110] do not typically include much nudity; some trans women similarly prefer to cover up [111] in the locker room.

          1.1.3.3.1.2. Pro:
          Trans women who aspire to pass [112] as women are particularly likely to make every effort to hide any indication of male anatomy.

          1.1.3.3.1.3. Pro:
          Women's facilities generally include private toilet stalls and changing rooms, which trans women can use to change, if desired (p. 159 [113]).

        1.1.3.3.2. Con:
        When given descriptions of the physical appearance of trans people, others tend to perceive them as belonging to their gender assigned at birth, rather than their chosen gender (p. 6 [114]).

          1.1.3.3.2.1. Con:
          The way that people perceive the appearance of actual humans is distinct from how they perceive descriptions of humans. One does not necessarily predict the other.

        1.1.3.3.3. Pro:
        The medical transition process [115] is essentially a set of procedures intended to alter a person's body to resemble that of a cis woman.

          1.1.3.3.3.1. Pro:
          Even trans women who do not undergo surgery engage in non-permanent tucking [116] procedures to hide their penises and testes.

          1.1.3.3.3.2. Con:
          Trans identity does not inherently involve surgical transition. Only 30% of trans women undergo some form of gender-confirming surgery so that their body more closely matches their identity (p. 187 [117]).

        1.1.3.3.4. Pro:
        Trans women often wear clothes, hairstyles, and accessories that are typically associated with women [118], even from a very young age [119]. Thus, they possess a physical appearance that is typically associated with women.

          1.1.3.3.4.1. Pro:
          Trans women often devote substantial time and energy to cultivating feminine body language, speech patterns, and mannerisms (pp. 55-56 [120]).

        1.1.3.3.5. Con:
        A female appearance is insufficient justification for access to women-only spaces, as even men can appear female if they so choose.

      1.1.3.4. Pro:
      Trans women often have notably different body types than cis women due to higher levels of adolescent testosterone exposure and the effects of hormone therapy (p. 1 [121]).

        1.1.3.4.1. Con:
        Expecting trans women to comply with patriarchal beauty standards for women's bodies is sexist.

          1.1.3.4.1.1. Con:
          There is a difference between appearing or being identifiable as female and looking feminine in a way that complies with social beauty standards.

        1.1.3.4.2. Pro:
        There are specific physical characteristics [122] by which trans women can be distinguished from cis women.

          1.1.3.4.2.1. Con:
          There are no physical characteristics [123] that can reliably be used to identify trans women; such beliefs have been associated with conservative anti-trans campaigns [124].

    1.1.4. Pro:
    Trans women may not experience certain biological processes that cis women experience.

      1.1.4.1. Pro:
      A lactation room [125] is a modern example of a women-only space which trans women would not need to access.

        1.1.4.1.1. Con:
        Not all women lactate, and not all who lactate are women, so the "women-only" label is inaccurate in this case.

          1.1.4.1.1.1. Pro:
          The term "lactation room" makes it clear that it is intended for a very specific audience: those who are lactating. There is no need to specify or verify someone's gender in order for them to access these spaces.

          1.1.4.1.1.2. Pro:
          Trans men are capable of lactating and chestfeeding [126] their infants.

          1.1.4.1.1.3. Pro:
          New medical developments [127] will likely enable trans women to breastfeed in the future.

          1.1.4.1.1.4. Con:
          Very few cis women are unable to lactate [128]; calling them out is unproductive.

          1.1.4.1.1.5. Pro:
          Some trans women are able to breastfeed their children [129] and would require access to a lactation room as would any other mother.

        1.1.4.1.2. Pro:
        Lactation rooms must be kept as a sanctuary to avoid misuse [130] or additional stress on mothers.

      1.1.4.2. Pro:
      Most trans women will never experience [131] menstruation in the strictest sense of the word.

        1.1.4.2.1. Con:
        Trans women undergoing hormone replacement treatment experience [132] cyclical period symptoms even if they do not technically menstruate.

        1.1.4.2.2. Pro:
        Menstrual pain can be as painful as a heart attack [133]. Some trans women might be unable to empathize with the intensity [134] of the menstrual pain that cis women experience.

        1.1.4.2.3. Pro:
        Period pain [135] can make it difficult for cis women to perform normally for an average of three to seven days per month. In comparison, trans women would not experience a decline in performance during those days.

          1.1.4.2.3.1. Pro:
          In companies where monthly performance and productivity metrics determine pay, reduced productivity caused by period pain [136] can cause cis women to lose out to trans women on monetary bonuses and promotions.

          1.1.4.2.3.2. Pro:
          Period pain [137] causes cis women to be less productive for a third of their time [138] at work.

          1.1.4.2.3.3. Con:
          Paid period leave [139] and similar accommodations can compensate for productivity losses caused by menstrual symptoms [140], suggesting that productivity and metrics are not as important as personal health and comfort.

          1.1.4.2.3.4. Con:
          Some trans women have been hospitalized over the severity of their period-like symptoms [141] while undergoing hormonal therapy.

        1.1.4.2.4. Con:
        While some women-only spaces are devoted to menstruation, the focus is generally on discussion [142] rather than the necessities of the biological process itself.

      1.1.4.3. Pro:
      Most trans women cannot get pregnant. Reproductive rights are often a core issue [143] in women-only spaces and represent a significant difference between trans and cis women.

        1.1.4.3.1. Pro:
        Women-only spaces that include trans women may find it more difficult to focus on reproductive rights, since trans women have no incentive to fight for those rights.

          1.1.4.3.1.1. Con:
          Trans individuals have consistently supported reproductive rights, despite the myth [144] to the contrary.

            1.1.4.3.1.1.1. Pro:
            The vast majority of LGBTQ+ non-profit organizations, including the National LGBTQ Task Force founded in 1973 [145], have been outspoken supporters of reproductive rights and justice [146] for all (p. 82 [147]).

            1.1.4.3.1.1.2. Con:
            Even if trans women support reproductive rights, such as abortion, they are less motivated to treat these concerns as central due to their inherent biological limitations.

            1.1.4.3.1.1.3. Pro:
            During Repeal the 8th in Ireland, feminists reported tremendous support [148] from trans women.

          1.1.4.3.1.2. Con:
          Reproductive rights are relevant to both cis and trans women.

            1.1.4.3.1.2.1. Pro:
            Central to the struggle for reproductive rights is pursuit of affordable and effective healthcare for women. Both trans and cis women benefit when improvements are made in this area (p. 1 [149]). As a result, trans and cis women are united, not divided, by the reproductive rights movement.

            1.1.4.3.1.2.2. Pro:
            Reproductive rights include the opportunity to pursue less common methods [150] of family planning, including surrogates and adoption. Both trans and cis women have needs for these options.

            1.1.4.3.1.2.3. Con:
            Even medically transitioned [151] trans women do not need birth control or abortion services; therefore, their biological limitations make their reproductive rights less relevant than those of cis women.

          1.1.4.3.1.3. Pro:
          Because the reproductive issues of trans women are different from those of cis women (p. 1 [152]), focusing on one set of issues will detract from the other.

            1.1.4.3.1.3.1. Con:
            Women's reproductive health clinics and providers of HRT for trans women have been systematically targeted [153] by the same groups using similar tactics.

            1.1.4.3.1.3.2. Pro:
            Cis women face euphemized reproductive violence and erasure that are directly linked to their reproductive capacity (p. 4 [154]).

          1.1.4.3.1.4. Pro:
          Women-only spaces for pregnant women are a response to patriarchal control of women's reproductive capacity [155]. Trans women have not been subjected to these same forces.

            1.1.4.3.1.4.1. Con:
            The struggle for reproductive rights and trans rights are united in their belief that all women should have autonomy over their own bodies [156]. As a result, trans and cis women can easily find common cause if they inhabit the same spaces.

            1.1.4.3.1.4.2. Pro:
            The abuse of women during childbirth at the hands of medical personnel remains common in many parts of the world (p. 1 [157]).

        1.1.4.3.2. Con:
        New medical procedures could enable trans women to get pregnant as soon as "tomorrow [158]."

          1.1.4.3.2.1. Con:
          The UK Human Fertilisation and Embryology Act 2008 makes it illegal for an in-vitro fertilization clinic to implant an embryo [159] in a trans woman.

          1.1.4.3.2.2. Pro:
          More than 23 babies have been born to women who have undergone uterine transplantation, and research suggests that the procedure could be feasible for trans women as well (pp. 350, 356 [160]).

          1.1.4.3.2.3. Con:
          Attempting to initiate a pregnancy in a transgender woman could pose an elevated risk to the fetus, which some experts believe would be unethical [161].

        1.1.4.3.3. Con:
        The relationship between a person’s gender and their body goes beyond reproductive functions (p. 99 [162]).

      1.1.4.4. Con:
      Not all women-only spaces focus on female biological processes. A majority are spaces to discuss [163] women's issues. Therefore, not allowing trans women, who also experience these issues, into such spaces invalidates their experiences as women and marginalizes them further.

        1.1.4.4.1. Con:
        Having the same genitalia could create a more intimate connection within a group if the conversation revolves around their experiences with genitals.

          1.1.4.4.1.1. Pro:
          Many people would feel more comfortable discussing genital insecurity [164] or physical dysphoria [165] with a group of people who have the same genitalia.

          1.1.4.4.1.2. Con:
          Conversations around genitalia tend to be best reserved for medical professionals.

        1.1.4.4.2. Pro:
        Women-only book clubs [166] are widespread and popular.

        1.1.4.4.3. Pro:
        Women-only makeup parties [167], tupperware parties [168], and other suburban household gatherings have empowered women and allowed them to discuss issues relevant to their realities.

        1.1.4.4.4. Con:
        The issues that are faced by cis women and trans women, although sharing elements in common, are distinct and should not be conflated [169].

      1.1.4.5. Con:
      The need for bathroom access is a universal human experience.

        1.1.4.5.1. Pro:
        The right to sanitation [170] without discrimination is recognized as a universal human right by the UN.

          1.1.4.5.1.1. Pro:
          The denial of this basic right contributes to mental health issues and increased marginalization of trans youth (pp. 31-32 [171]).

          1.1.4.5.1.2. Con:
          Segregation of bathrooms by sex is a form of protection [172], not discrimination.

          1.1.4.5.1.3. Con:
          Trans people are not being denied access to sanitation facilities; they are being denied the ability to choose which facilities they can use. They still have facilities available for use.

        1.1.4.5.2. Con:
        While access might be a universal need, the forms and options for access might not be universally equitable. While a urinal might be available for a woman, it would not be a useful substitute for a toilet.

      1.1.4.6. Con:
      If biological processes define cis women, then it would be possible to unreasonably exclude many members who do not experience these processes.

        1.1.4.6.1. Pro:
        Many cis women do not menstruate [173].

          1.1.4.6.1.1. Pro:
          Physical issues, such as PCOS [174], excessive exercise, or nutritional imbalances, and psychological issues, including stress, can disrupt [175] menstrual cycles without actual bleeding in cis women.

          1.1.4.6.1.2. Con:
          Many of the causes [176] for women not menstruating are medical or psychosomatic conditions that require intervention. This demonstrates that menstruation is a typical characteristic of cis women, and that not having a period is a deviation from the norm.

          1.1.4.6.1.3. Pro:
          Cis women do not menstruate before puberty [177] or after menopause [178].

            1.1.4.6.1.3.1. Pro:
            Postmenopausal women experience a decline in fertility and typically no longer experience menstruation [179]. This does not mean that these women should not be able to access women-only spaces.

            1.1.4.6.1.3.2. Con:
            This comparison is invalid since these cis women will experience or have already experienced many years of menstruation in a way that trans women do not.

          1.1.4.6.1.4. Pro:
          Cis women on certain forms of birth control [180] do not menstruate.

          1.1.4.6.1.5. Pro:
          Women who have undergone a hysterectomy cease menstruation [181].

        1.1.4.6.2. Pro:
        Some cis women are unable to bear children.

          1.1.4.6.2.1. Pro:
          Women who have undergone a hysterectomy cannot become pregnant [182].

          1.1.4.6.2.2. Pro:
          Approximately one in five women [183] in the US between 15 and 49 have undergone female sterilization.

          1.1.4.6.2.3. Con:
          A cis woman who is unable to bear children would not be excluded from a women-only space based on that criterion alone.

            1.1.4.6.2.3.1. Con:
            A support space for young mothers would be unlikely to accept a woman without children.

          1.1.4.6.2.4. Pro:
          Natural and external causes can render women infertile [184] through no fault of their own.

        1.1.4.6.3. Con:
        Since cis women are defined as women whose gender matches their biological sex [185] assigned at birth, their biological features are an essential characteristic of who they are.

          1.1.4.6.3.1. Con:
          Transgender and cisgender identities are both equally defined by a relationship between an individual's personal sense of their gender and their sex assigned at birth. Neither group has a greater or lesser biological basis than the other.

          1.1.4.6.3.2. Pro:
          Since 98% or more of people are cisgender (p. 1 [186]), "woman" and "female" are often treated as synonymous in common usage.

        1.1.4.6.4. Con:
        While not all cis women experience these biological functions, their lives have been affected by them from a very young age.

          1.1.4.6.4.1. Con:
          The lives of trans women are also affected by these biological processes, even if they do not experience them. For example, many trans women experience dysphoria about pregnancy and menstruation [187].

          1.1.4.6.4.2. Pro:
          The heavy stigmatization [188] of menstruation makes it difficult for cis women to discuss related issues in public and in the open, suggesting that they need women-only spaces to hold these discussions.

            1.1.4.6.4.2.1. Con:
            The inclusion of trans women in private women-only spaces where cis women feel comfortable discussing menstruation would not make these spaces open or public.

            1.1.4.6.4.2.2. Pro:
            Forty-two percent [189] of US women say they experience period shame, and nearly 60% of women feel embarrassed when they menstruate.

          1.1.4.6.4.3. Pro:
          All cis women are affected by childbirth. Those who have children are affected by pregnancy and motherhood, whereas those without children have to deal with societal judgement.

            1.1.4.6.4.3.1. Con:
            Attitudes toward childless and childfree women are changing, as evidenced by recent findings which suggest that there are no significant differences [190] in social perceptions of women who choose not to have children and mothers.

      1.1.4.7. Pro:
      Women-only spaces that are focused on biological processes are subject to greater scrutiny than those that are not, as there is an agreed-upon "special sense of privacy" in such spaces (pp. 95-96 [191]).

        1.1.4.7.1. Pro:
        Some cis women construe the presence of trans women in spaces that are focused on biological processes as a violation of their privacy [192].

        1.1.4.7.2. Con:
        Forcing trans women to use men's facilities violates their privacy [193].

      1.1.4.8. Con:
      Due to physiological differences and medical conditions [194], there is great variation in the biological processes experienced by cis women.

        1.1.4.8.1. Pro:
        Women's frequency of urination varies greatly, particularly if conditions such as pregnancy and overactive bladder syndrome [195] are considered.

        1.1.4.8.2. Con:
        There is greater variability [196] in the biological processes of cis and trans women then there is between different cis women.

  1.2. Con:
  The inclusion of anyone who identifies as a woman into women-only spaces will make them less safe.

    1.2.1. Con:
    The purpose of women-only spaces is to provide safety for women (p. 1 [197]); it doesn't make sense to sacrifice the safety of trans women in order to protect cis women.

      1.2.1.1. Con:
      Trans women needing safe spaces does not imply they should be granted access to women-only spaces.

        1.2.1.1.1. Con:
        Given that there are far fewer trans women than cis women, it may be more difficult to have a dedicated space for trans women due to a lack of funding or members (p. 1 [198]).

          1.2.1.1.1.1. Con:
          Since trans women are a minority in society and lack their own spaces, groups specifically supporting trans women would likely attract an influx of people in search of help and like-minded individuals.

          1.2.1.1.1.2. Con:
          The only homeless service provider that explicitly caters to LGBTQ+ young people in Melbourne, Australia has stated that they could multiply their efforts tenfold [199] and still receive referrals. This demonstrates that there are not enough LGBTQ+ spaces available to meet the demand.

        1.2.1.1.2. Pro:
        Unfortunately, many women-only spaces are not effective safe spaces [200]; trans women would be safer in well-planned, inclusive safe spaces [201].

      1.2.1.2. Pro:
      The opinions of those who oppose inclusive spaces are less important than the legitimate safety concerns [202] of trans people.

        1.2.1.2.1. Con:
        Cis women might find it emotionally distressing [203] to come across a person they perceive to be of another gender in a women-only space.

          1.2.1.2.1.1. Pro:
          According to interviews, the inclusion of trans women in Hampstead Heath Pond made some of the regular users of the swimming facility feel uncomfortable and unsafe [204].

            1.2.1.2.1.1.1. Con:
            Trans women should not be penalised because some cis women feel uncomfortable around them. Instead, such women should make an effort to challenge [205] why trans individuals make them feel uncomfortable.

            1.2.1.2.1.1.2. Con:
            Purely anecdotal evidence is of little use in creating general principles. In reality, empirical evidence suggests that cis men rather than cis women are more concerned about the safety of cis women around trans women (p. 1 [206]).

            1.2.1.2.1.1.3. Pro:
            The pond is used by girls as young as 8 years old [207]; some parents may feel that their child is too young to fully understand trans identity [208] at this age.

            1.2.1.2.1.1.4. Pro:
            There is also a mixed pond and a men's pond at Hampstead Heath [209]. Pre-transition trans women are already able to use these facilities.

            1.2.1.2.1.1.5. Con:
            If the same were to be said of any other physical difference, such as ethnicity or disability status, this would be considered discrimination.

          1.2.1.2.1.2. Pro:
          Women may feel uncomfortable and unsafe when they see a person who appears male in a women's bathroom, regardless of that person's gender (p. 10 [210]).

            1.2.1.2.1.2.1. Pro:
            Trans men who were AFAB but have a visibly male appearance have caused a great deal of commotion [211] when using women's bathrooms.

            1.2.1.2.1.2.2. Con:
            Responsibility for such discomfort lies with the legislators who have introduced bathroom bills that require people to use the bathroom of their sex assigned at birth. Due to this lack of foresight [212], transgender men have often been forced to use women's restrooms.

            1.2.1.2.1.2.3. Pro:
            When people that are perceived as male use women's restrooms, it can make women so uncomfortable that they stop using the restrooms altogether (p. 2 [213]).

          1.2.1.2.1.3. Pro:
          Given that the primary purpose of some women-only spaces is to enable women to feel safer or more comfortable, the perception of risk, whether justified or not, is a real issue.

            1.2.1.2.1.3.1. Pro:
            Survivors of sexual assault suffering from post-traumatic stress disorder [214] (PTSD) may seek spaces that are free from male bodies to avoid assault-related triggers [215].

              1.2.1.2.1.3.1.1. Con:
              Given that every incidence of violence is different, accounting for everyone's triggers is impossible.

                1.2.1.2.1.3.1.1.1. Con:
                Avoiding common triggers where possible is essential in certain spaces, such as sexual assault support groups, where people can and should be protected against obvious triggers.

                1.2.1.2.1.3.1.1.2. Pro:
                Many other factors, including the scents and sounds [216] associated with an act of violence, can be triggering to victims.

              1.2.1.2.1.3.1.2. Con:
              Women only need to be free from the threat of assault; this has nothing to do with the person's genitalia. This is why women feel very safe around gay men [217].

              1.2.1.2.1.3.1.3. Pro:
              Many female rape survivors find women-only spaces, where they are with cis women who fully understand and share their particular experience of womanhood, to be incredibly important for their recovery.

                1.2.1.2.1.3.1.3.1. Con:
                Rape affects each woman differently depending on their race, social or economic status, or mental status (p. 392 [218]). Thus, no two cis women may ever be able to fully comprehend the uniqueness of each others' traumatic experiences.

                1.2.1.2.1.3.1.3.2. Con:
                A trans woman would only require access to spaces for rape survivors if she herself had been raped. Therefore, she is just as likely as her cis counterparts to understand the experience of sexual assault.

                  1.2.1.2.1.3.1.3.2.1. Pro:
                  These groups are beneficial because they allow victims to share their experiences and feelings and relate to those of others (p. 7 [219]). Sharing these experiences with trans women will not diminish the benefits.

                1.2.1.2.1.3.1.3.3. Pro:
                The 2010 Equality Act [220] protects transgender people from discrimination and exclusion from single-sex facilities with one exception: group counselling sessions for women who are sexual assault victims. In such groups, a trans person AMAB can be excluded if their presence would make it unlikely for other women to attend.

                  1.2.1.2.1.3.1.3.3.1. Con:
                  Though the law currently permits discrimination and exclusion in certain cases, this does not mean it is morally justified. The law may change in the future and prohibit such exclusionary acts.

                  1.2.1.2.1.3.1.3.3.2. Pro:
                  The fact that this exception was created demonstrates that female rape victims have specific needs in the women-only groups in which they participate.

                1.2.1.2.1.3.1.3.4. Con:
                There is no documented proof that including trans women in these spaces prevents this support from happening.

              1.2.1.2.1.3.1.4. Pro:
              Survivors of sexual violence should have the right to create their own healing and support spaces in whatever way is most beneficial to their recovery.

                1.2.1.2.1.3.1.4.1. Pro:
                If a woman feels that she needs a space free from male genitalia to heal from trauma, it is unethical to tell her that her feelings are wrong [221].

                1.2.1.2.1.3.1.4.2. Con:
                Since avoidance is not an effective way [222] to treat PTSD, it would be more beneficial to provide people with support to overcome [223] their triggers.

              1.2.1.2.1.3.1.5. Pro:
              Approximately 99% [224] of rape and sexual assault perpetrators are biologically male, making spaces without penises substantially safer.

                1.2.1.2.1.3.1.5.1. Con:
                Trans women who undergo gender-affirming surgery [225] have their male genitalia removed and reconstructed to resemble female genitalia.

                1.2.1.2.1.3.1.5.2. Con:
                The need for spaces that are "safe from penises" is unreasonable, since many parts of the body can be used for acts of violence. The official FBI definition of rape includes penetration of any part of the body, making no distinction [226] between body parts, or even foreign objects.

                1.2.1.2.1.3.1.5.3. Pro:
                -> See 1.1.2.1.3.1.

              1.2.1.2.1.3.1.6. Con:
              The absence of male genitalia is not what makes women-only spaces safe and supportive for women.

                1.2.1.2.1.3.1.6.1. Pro:
                Women-only safe spaces exist as a form of protection against harassment and violence, particularly that of a sexual nature, which happens to women regardless of their genitalia (p. 5 [227]).

                1.2.1.2.1.3.1.6.2. Pro:
                A safe and supportive women-only space allows women to be free to express their authentic selves, which trans women may be unable to do in many other circumstances (p. 7 [228]).

                1.2.1.2.1.3.1.6.3. Con:
                To a survivor of male-perpetrated rape, a penis is not comparable to other parts of the body; victims of sexual assault may perceive penises as weapons [229] that have been used to intimidate, punish or assert dominance over them.

                  1.2.1.2.1.3.1.6.3.1. Pro:
                  Being assaulted with a penis is substantively different from being assaulted with another body part since rape survivors [230] experience profound stigma, which is not the case for victims of non-sexual assault.

                  1.2.1.2.1.3.1.6.3.2. Con:
                  There are many similarities between being attacked by another part of the body and a penis; the effects of the former cannot be said to be any less damaging than those of the latter, as demonstrated by the literature on corporal punishment [231] and domestic abuse [232].

            1.2.1.2.1.3.2. Con:
            Women should find a way to cope with such feelings in a way that does not hamper the right of trans women to use these spaces.

            1.2.1.2.1.3.3. Con:
            Reinforcing an inaccurate assessment of risk does a disservice to both cis and trans women (p. 12 [233]).

          1.2.1.2.1.4. Con:
          Gender perception is highly subjective [234] and can vary widely depending on the observer.

          1.2.1.2.1.5. Con:
          The majority of cis women express positive views of trans women [235], indicating that they may be fine with encountering trans women in women-only spaces.

            1.2.1.2.1.5.1. Con:
            Positive views towards trans women are not the same as arguing for trans inclusion. A cis woman could hold generally positive views about trans women yet remain opposed to their inclusion in women-only spaces.

            1.2.1.2.1.5.2. Pro:
            There is a great deal of anti-trans rhetoric that attempts to create the false appearance of heated opposition between cis and trans women.

          1.2.1.2.1.6. Con:
          The emotional distress of transphobic people is not a valid reason to discriminate against trans people.

            1.2.1.2.1.6.1. Con:
            Prohibiting discrimination based on gender identity undermines the work women have done to fight discrimination [236] based on their sex, specifically their reproductive capacity.

            1.2.1.2.1.6.2. Pro:
            Trans women might be equally distressed at having their identity completely disregarded because of another person's uninformed perceptions.

        1.2.1.2.2. Pro:
        Despite legal protections, more trans women are murdered [237] in Brazil than anywhere else, putting them particularly at risk in the South American country.

        1.2.1.2.3. Pro:
        In 2021, more than 57 transgender or gender-nonconforming people were violently killed [238] in the US.

        1.2.1.2.4. Con:
        Cis women deserve the right to create and maintain their own exclusive spaces.

          1.2.1.2.4.1. Pro:
          Cis women, like all groups, should have the right to assemble and associate based upon shared characteristics like biological sex or sexual orientation (p. 14 [239]).

      1.2.1.3. Pro:
      Excluding trans women would force them to use men's spaces. This can be detrimental to their health and well-being and alarming to men when a person who identifies as a woman enters their space.

        1.2.1.3.1. Pro:
        Transitioning socially, as well as medically, greatly reduces symptoms of dysphoria [240], especially in adolescents.

        1.2.1.3.2. Pro:
        Trans women allocated to men-only prison blocks are at much greater risk [241] of injury or death.

        1.2.1.3.3. Con:
        Some men may be welcoming or indifferent to the presence of a person who identifies as a woman in men-only spaces.

        1.2.1.3.4. Con:
        Similarly, some cis women would be alarmed by the presence of a trans woman with masculine features in a women-only space; there is no reason why men should be kept comfortable at women's expense.

      1.2.1.4. Con:
      Spaces that include cis women are unlikely to be as safe as spaces reserved exclusively for trans women.

        1.2.1.4.1. Pro:
        If trans women are allowed into women-only spaces despite the protests of existing members, as in the case of Hampstead Heath Pond [242], they are likely to face hostility and animosity from those members. As a result, such spaces would not be effective safe spaces for trans women.

          1.2.1.4.1.1. Con:
          Hostility and animosity may only be an immediate short-term reaction. Changes are often met with hostility [243], but opinions will likely change as people become accustomed to the new normal.

            1.2.1.4.1.1.1. Pro:
            The law that required seat belt use [244] in cars was first met with widespread protest but was eventually adopted by the general public.

          1.2.1.4.1.2. Con:
          Trans women have been welcome at the Kenwood Ladies’ Pond in Hampstead Heath since 2010 [245]; trans women have overwhelmingly been able to use the pond in safety and without hostility.

          1.2.1.4.1.3. Pro:
          Cis women making trans women feel unsafe or uncomfortable completely defeats the purpose of a safe space for women.

        1.2.1.4.2. Pro:
        There have been reports [246] of trans women being assaulted [247] by cis women in women-only spaces.

        1.2.1.4.3. Con:
        Even if cis-inclusive safe spaces are less safe for trans women than trans-exclusive spaces, they are safer than the lack of access to any gendered space whatsoever.

      1.2.1.5. Pro:
      Trans women suffer from violence at the hands of strangers and their partners.

        1.2.1.5.1. Pro:
        A significant number [248] of trans women are victims of hate crimes and domestic abuse from a partner.

        1.2.1.5.2. Con:
        Trans women face a similar [249] risk of gendered violence as cis woman because they are treated equally by a patriarchal society.

          1.2.1.5.2.1. Con:
          Trans women are four times more likely [250] to be victims of violent crime compared to cisgender women.

        1.2.1.5.3. Pro:
        Homeless shelters are generally unsafe for trans women.

          1.2.1.5.3.1. Pro:
          Trans women who seek shelter from intimate partner violence don't receive equal protection [251] from staff members at women-only shelters and crisis centers, who regard them as a threat to the comfort of survivors and disruptive of the female-empowerment model.

          1.2.1.5.3.2. Con:
          Homeless shelters are notoriously violent places [252], independent of sex or gender.

          1.2.1.5.3.3. Pro:
          The Human Resource Council found that 52% of transgender people who stayed at a shelter were verbally harassed and/or physically/sexually assaulted (p. 51 [253]).

        1.2.1.5.4. Pro:
        In 2017, one in eight [254] transgender people was physically attacked by a colleague or customer within the past year.

      1.2.1.6. Con:
      Not all women-only spaces are intended for safety purposes; many spaces serve a specific functional purpose in the interest of fairness or to help uplift women and put a spotlight on their achievements.

        1.2.1.6.1. Pro:
        Some women-only spaces are professional groups focused on business and networking [255], including women-only coworking spaces [256].

        1.2.1.6.2. Con:
        Women-only spaces that serve a functional purpose should be available to all who can make use of the intended purpose of the space.

          1.2.1.6.2.1. Pro:
          Women-only spaces that promote networking and career advancement should be open to trans women, as they face an even wider wage gap [257] than cis women and may see their earnings fall by up to a third after transitioning.

          1.2.1.6.2.2. Con:
          This would be unreasonable in many cases. For example, this would mean that men should be allowed to use women's bathrooms, since they can make use of the space.

        1.2.1.6.3. Con:
        Any space reserved exclusively for women is a safe space by default, as it excludes men who could potentially harm them.

          1.2.1.6.3.1. Con:
          Women are as capable as men of perpetrating violence and harm against other women.

            1.2.1.6.3.1.1. Con:
            Nearly all violence [258] and especially fatal attacks [259] against women are carried out by men, not other women.

          1.2.1.6.3.2. Con:
          Despite the space being reserved for women, there are reports of men assaulting women in bathrooms [260].

          1.2.1.6.3.3. Pro:
          The increased attention focused on any man attempting to access a women-only space acts as a deterrent to crime.

          1.2.1.6.3.4. Pro:
          Sexual assault prevention strategies have utilized women-only spaces to provide women with access to targeted resources [261] that can discreetly help them out of uncomfortable and potentially dangerous situations.

        1.2.1.6.4. Pro:
        Women's sports are separate from men's in the interest of fairness and competition [262], not safety.

          1.2.1.6.4.1. Con:
          There could be a safety risk for women in some sports, like boxing [263] and wrestling, if they were mixed gender.

          1.2.1.6.4.2. Pro:
          Some sports are mixed-gender [264], where physical differences do not impact the spirit or quality of competition.

    1.2.2. Pro:
    Cis women could be placed at additional risk [265] in women-only spaces due to the inclusion of trans women.

      1.2.2.1. Con:
      Trans women are not in any way responsible for acts of violence committed by men. This misconception springs from anti-trans prejudice.

        1.2.2.1.1. Pro:
        Attempting to portray trans women as predatory men is a debunked anti-trans scare tactic [266].

        1.2.2.1.2. Con:
        If trans women were to gain access to women-only spaces despite the voiced concerns of many cis women [267], and this resulted in increased violence to cis women by men in such spaces, then some would consider trans women as having enabled these crimes.

      1.2.2.2. Con:
      In many nations, progressive legislation has allowed individuals who identify as trans women to access women-only spaces. There is no link [268] between allowing transgender people to use facilities that match their identity and violence against cis women.

        1.2.2.2.1. Con:
        On physical prowess [269] indices, trans women outperform cis women [270]. Therefore, to the extent that women fear men [271] because of their capacity to overpower them [272], women's fear of being assaulted by trans women is justified.

        1.2.2.2.2. Pro:
        The trans community would certainly band together [273] to put a stop to any increase in violence perpetrated by trans women in order to preserve the progress made in fighting for their rights and acceptance in society.

        1.2.2.2.3. Pro:
        There are no documented instances [274] of trans women harassing or assaulting cis women in women-only spaces.

          1.2.2.2.3.1. Con:
          Cis women who have been assaulted by trans women may find it more difficult to get justice, as charges against the offending trans woman may be perceived as a transphobic attack [275] on all trans women.

          1.2.2.2.3.2. Pro:
          Police, school officials, and government representatives all agree that trans women do not pose a threat [276] to cis women in public bathrooms.

        1.2.2.2.4. Pro:
        Excluding trans women from women's spaces implies that trans women are predatory, whereas most evidence [277] suggests that they are more likely to be at risk themselves [278], especially in men-only spaces.

          1.2.2.2.4.1. Pro:
          Predator arguments are not based in fact, but are used by demagogues to scapegoat [279] gender minorities for political purposes.

          1.2.2.2.4.2. Pro:
          Trans women are more likely to be sexually assaulted [280] or harassed than to sexually assault or harass someone else.

          1.2.2.2.4.3. Pro:
          Transgender individuals face particularly high rates of public discrimination (pp. 4, 198 [281]).

          1.2.2.2.4.4. Con:
          There are other reasons why some people seek to exclude trans women from women-only spaces, such as a belief in gender essentialism [282].

          1.2.2.2.4.5. Con:
          In some cases, the inclusion of trans women in a women-only space can be detrimental to the intended purpose of the space; this is simply pragmatism and does not imply anything negative about trans women.

        1.2.2.2.5. Con:
        A trans woman in the UK allegedly used transitioning as a deceptive tactic [283] to assault other inmates in a women-only prison block.

        1.2.2.2.6. Con:
        Cis women who oppose trans women entering women-only spaces are often subjected to threats of violence [284] from militant trans activists.

          1.2.2.2.6.1. Pro:
          Misgendering [285] can cause trans activists to react with violence.

            1.2.2.2.6.1.1. Pro:
            A trans athlete was jailed for 18 years for the attempted murder [286] of the official who questioned her competition status as a woman.

            1.2.2.2.6.1.2. Pro:
            A right-wing reporter was assaulted and threatened on live TV [287] for intentionally misgendering a participant in the discussion.

              1.2.2.2.6.1.2.1. Con:
              Oftentimes, right-wing extremists are openly and unabashedly hostile [288] towards trans rights, intentionally provoking trans people to get a colorful response.

            1.2.2.2.6.1.3. Con:
            Claims of harassment are sometimes invented by anti-trans groups [289] to make trans people look bad.

            1.2.2.2.6.1.4. Con:
            Offending anyone can cause them to react with violence; these situations are no different from any other.

              1.2.2.2.6.1.4.1. Pro:
              Gender-based obscenities are commonplace [290]; referring to a heterosexual man with pejoratives that make him appear effeminate is likely to cause a fight.

          1.2.2.2.6.2. Pro:
          In 2017, a trans activist was fined £150 [291] for assaulting a gender-critical feminist at a demonstration in Hyde Park.

            1.2.2.2.6.2.1. Con:
            One trans activist being fined for assault is simply anecdotal evidence of what might be a statistically insignificant phenomenon.

              1.2.2.2.6.2.1.1. Con:
              On International Women's Day 2022 [292], there were attacks against feminist demonstrators carried out by trans activists in multiple countries.

          1.2.2.2.6.3. Con:
          Most trans people do not support violence; as a group, they are disproportionately affected [293] by violence and are acutely aware of the harm it causes.

            1.2.2.2.6.3.1. Con:
            Violence can be a method to spur social change [294] in an effort to gain equality.

            1.2.2.2.6.3.2. Pro:
            The best way [295] to deal with hate crimes against trans people is to focus on raising awareness and generating a systematic response, instead of creating further animosity.

            1.2.2.2.6.3.3. Pro:
            Transgender individuals [296] are among the most vulnerable [297] in society; nearly nine out of ten trans people have experienced gender-based violence.

          1.2.2.2.6.4. Con:
          Isolated examples of threats of violence against cis women with no overarching pattern do not demonstrate that all trans women who desire to enter women-only spaces would threaten violence.

      1.2.2.3. Pro:
      There is evidence suggesting that trans women offend [298] at the same rate as men as the pattern of both overall crime and violent crime in trans women follows a male pattern of criminality (p. 6 [299]).

        1.2.2.3.1. Con:
        Acts of voyeurism, which represent a threat to women, are typically committed by men [300] and not trans women. The latter group should not be punished for the offenses of the former.

        1.2.2.3.2. Con:
        Suggesting that trans women offend at the same rate as men is a misrepresentation of the results of the study [301], which did not examine the likelihood to commit violent crime [302] (p. 7 [303]).

          1.2.2.3.2.1. Pro:
          With regards to criminality, the study divides the cohort into two groups. For the 1989-2003 group, there is no male pattern of criminality [304] among trans women.

          1.2.2.3.2.2. Con:
          The study author specifies that the study looked at conviction rates, not conviction type [305]. This means that trans women and cis men were convicted at a similar rate in the 1973-1989 group, but there is no data on the type of crimes of which they were convicted.

        1.2.2.3.3. Pro:
        According to UK Ministry of Justice statistics, in 2017 and 2019, approximately half of the transgender prisoners in England and Wales had at least one conviction for a sexual offense [306].

      1.2.2.4. Pro:
      -> See 1.1.1.2.5.1.

      1.2.2.5. Con:
      Exclusion by cis women poses a much more serious threat [307] to the health and safety of trans women than the inclusion of trans women poses to cis women.

        1.2.2.5.1. Pro:
        There are significantly more trans women who would be hurt or put in danger by being excluded (p. 147 [308]) than there are men willing to pretend to be trans women in order to get into women's spaces (p. 1 [309]).

        1.2.2.5.2. Con:
        Holding cis women responsible for the violence perpetrated by men against trans women is unfair.

    1.2.3. Con:
    Women-only spaces can make use of trained security staff or guards to maintain safety.

      1.2.3.1. Con:
      Women-only spaces are often either publicly funded or voluntary spaces. As a result, they often lack the resources needed to introduce the level of policing that would be required to keep these spaces safe for the people who use them.

        1.2.3.1.1. Pro:
        The inability of women-only spaces to provide sufficient security to keep the spaces safe and usable for women means that neither cis nor trans women can safely access these spaces. This is a lose-lose situation.

        1.2.3.1.2. Con:
        The increased use of video surveillance allows restricted spaces, such as women-only spaces, to be monitored economically.

          1.2.3.1.2.1. Con:
          Increased use of video surveillance creates privacy issues [310], especially regarding who is responsible for watching the cameras and where the footage is stored.

      1.2.3.2. Pro:
      Any women-only space that is intended to function as a WGSS (Women and Girls Safe Space) must include security guards specifically hired to ensure the safety of both patrons and employees (p. 218 [311]).

        1.2.3.2.1. Con:
        In the US, 75% of security officers are men [312]; it may be difficult for women-only spaces to find women who are trained security officers.

          1.2.3.2.1.1. Pro:
          Having men working as security guards in a women-only space is problematic since it is contrary to the purpose of the space and can place women at risk.

          1.2.3.2.1.2. Con:
          Security officers are extensively vetted [313] before being hired, and many hold government security clearances. As their only function is to protect the space, their gender should not matter.

          1.2.3.2.1.3. Pro:
          Men should be prohibited from working as security guards within women-only safe spaces unless women guards are unavailable, and their physical access to the space should be strictly limited to after-hours operations (p. 215 [314]).

        1.2.3.2.2. Pro:
        Security staff can enforce rules, de-escalate tense or potentially violent situations, remove disruptive or dangerous individuals, and contact law enforcement as needed.

      1.2.3.3. Con:
      In some cases, security officers themselves have assaulted trans women [315] legally accessing women-only spaces.

        1.2.3.3.1. Pro:
        Regardless of whether trans women are allowed to access a women-only space, some security guards will likely remain opposed and seek to deny them access.

        1.2.3.3.2. Con:
        The failure of some security guards to honor the rules of access is justification for replacing them with better guards, not for excluding trans women.

      1.2.3.4. Pro:
      Staff in shelters or crisis service centers risk assess [316] every single person, including cis women. There is little safety risk in providing services to trans women.

        1.2.3.4.1. Pro:
        Shelters for at-risk women often have existing partnerships with law enforcement and government agencies [317] to ensure safety and security.

        1.2.3.4.2. Con:
        Relying on personal assessment of risk is often insufficient, as risk assessment practices are not standardized [318] and can vary widely in different jurisdictions or between individual staff.

    1.2.4. Pro:
    Men may abuse the self-identification protocols in order to harm those who do genuinely identify as women in women-only spaces.

      1.2.4.1. Con:
      Allowing those who identify as women into women-only spaces would not grant access to cis men, because, unlike trans women, they do not identify as women.

        1.2.4.1.1. Pro:
        Perpetuating the idea that women need to be protected from men at all costs contributes to toxic masculinity [319] by implying that all men are inherently dangerous.

        1.2.4.1.2. Con:
        Because gender identity is a personal experience, it is not always apparent to others and may be difficult to verify without blatantly requesting that a person explicitly reveal their gender identity.

          1.2.4.1.2.1. Pro:
          Emerging concepts of gender fluidity [320] present an additional challenge to women-only spaces, as someone can identify as a woman or as a man at different times.

        1.2.4.1.3. Pro:
        A man accessing a women-only space under the guise of being a woman is simply a liar and potentially a pervert, not a man who identifies as a woman.

        1.2.4.1.4. Pro:
        The argument that allowing trans women into women-only spaces will also permit men to enter such spaces is a slippery slope [321] fallacy.

          1.2.4.1.4.1. Pro:
          Men are unlikely to want to use women-only spaces such as bathrooms and gyms.

            1.2.4.1.4.1.1. Pro:
            In some cases, men's amenities are better [322] than the facilities provided for women.

            1.2.4.1.4.1.2. Con:
            Concerns about men accessing women-only spaces have little to do with a lack of similar facilities and more to do with threats to the safety of women.

          1.2.4.1.4.2. Con:
          Access policies based on self-identification do enable men to access women-only spaces by claiming to identify as women, regardless of whether they choose to do so. Since the mechanism by which this occurs is clear, this is not a slippery slope [323].

      1.2.4.2. Pro:
      Sexual predators could take advantage of inclusive policies by posing as members of a different gender in order to gain access to these facilities and harm others.

        1.2.4.2.1. Pro:
        There are reports of abusive behavior [324] by men pretending to be trans women accessing women-only spaces in order to prey on vulnerable women.

          1.2.4.2.1.1. Con:
          Someone pretending to be transgender that commits an act of sexual violence in a women-only space is already subject to all relevant laws on sexual assault [325].

            1.2.4.2.1.1.1. Pro:
            Since police would charge such a person with the same crimes [326] if they entered the bathroom without pretending to be a woman, trans-exclusionary laws and policies do not create any additional safety.

          1.2.4.2.1.2. Pro:
          Sexual assault against women [327] increased significantly following the publication of Target’s gender-inclusion policy in April 2016.

            1.2.4.2.1.2.1. Con:
            Awareness of sexual crimes [328] in society has been on the rise over the last two decades; recently, women have become more inclined to report sexual offenses. As such, increased reports of assault may be unrelated to the establishment of gender-inclusive policies.

            1.2.4.2.1.2.2. Pro:
            In all of the 220 media-reported incidents, the perpetrators were male [329], while 94.5% of the victims were female.

              1.2.4.2.1.2.2.1. Con:
              The assaults in Target are in line with the national statistics [330] on perpetrators and victims of sexual assault; they do not show a deviation from society at large.

              1.2.4.2.1.2.2.2. Pro:
              Some of the 220 perpetrators plausibly could have been cis men posing as trans women.

                1.2.4.2.1.2.2.2.1. Con:
                The study does not include any information on the actual or claimed gender of the perpetrators, so any assertion about cis male predators posing as trans is unfounded.

            1.2.4.2.1.2.3. Pro:
            There were more instances of sexual assault in Target locations in 2016 and 2017 [331] than in prior years.

              1.2.4.2.1.2.3.1. Con:
              The number of such incidents increased each year [332] between 2007 and 2017, indicating that this trend predates Target's gender-inclusion policy.

            1.2.4.2.1.2.4. Con:
            The cited study is biased, flawed, and has significant conflicts of interest.

              1.2.4.2.1.2.4.1. Pro:
              The methodology of the study is flawed because it relies on media reports [333] during a period of moral panic [334] instead of individual incident reports.

              1.2.4.2.1.2.4.2. Pro:
              The study author, Paul Dirks [335], is head of the "WOMAN Means Something" campaign against [336] the inclusion of trans people in women-only spaces. As such, the study suffers from a significant conflict of interest that damages the legitimacy of the conclusions.

                1.2.4.2.1.2.4.2.1. Con:
                The cited website is not anti-trans but pro-cis women [337]. Speaking up for the rights of cis women does not mean one is undermining the rights of trans women.

                  1.2.4.2.1.2.4.2.1.1. Con:
                  The language on the site refers to trans women as "strange male(s) [338]," which is transphobic, derogatory, and denies their gender identity.

                  1.2.4.2.1.2.4.2.1.2. Pro:
                  There is a difference between seeking to protect women from male predators and seeking to exclude trans people.

                  1.2.4.2.1.2.4.2.1.3. Con:
                  The argument that allowing trans women to access womens' bathrooms will lead to predatory men entering to commit crimes is a debunked anti-trans tactic [339]; this is a slippery slope fallacy [340] that is unsupported [341] by the available evidence.

                1.2.4.2.1.2.4.2.2. Pro:
                The Target study makes no mention of an attempt to avoid bias resulting from a conflict of interest, which is a key element of scientific study (p. 1 [342]). Therefore, the study findings must be called into question.

              1.2.4.2.1.2.4.3. Con:
              Regardless of the study's limitations, there is sufficient evidence to point to an issue that deserves additional scrutiny (p. 1 [343]).

          1.2.4.2.1.3. Pro:
          In 2012, a predatory man falsely claiming to be a trans woman assaulted two women [344] in Toronto women's shelters.

            1.2.4.2.1.3.1. Con:
            The predator in question had sexually assaulted other women [345] in the past without claiming to be transgender; trans-inclusive policies were not cited as a contributing factor to those attacks.

          1.2.4.2.1.4. Pro:
          Cis men can take advantage of bathroom bills to expose themselves [346] to women by claiming their gender identity is trans.

            1.2.4.2.1.4.1. Con:
            Predatory cis men can, and do, enter women-only spaces in order to expose themselves [347] regardless of gender-inclusive policies.

          1.2.4.2.1.5. Con:
          A small handful [348] of problematic individuals should not be allowed to undermine sincere efforts to make women-only spaces more inclusive or prevent trans people from accessing them.

            1.2.4.2.1.5.1. Con:
            The potential damage that could be caused by a small number of men in women-only spaces is massive, including sexual assault, harassment, and stalking. Even if such events are rare, their impact on women's lives is enormous.

              1.2.4.2.1.5.1.1. Con:
              This risk is not exclusive to women-only spaces. Sexual assault [349], harassment, and stalking can happen in any space. Therefore, this argument is irrelevant.

            1.2.4.2.1.5.2. Con:
