            A few incidents of abuse and the resulting fear of abuse undermine the feelings of safety and protection that these spaces are meant to provide to women. In many cases, the feeling of safety is the primary reason that women seek out these spaces in the first place (p. 7 [350]).

              1.2.4.2.1.5.2.1. Con:
              Removing the rights of trans women in order to protect cis women from potential harm is a paternalistic [351] practice. Preserving the rights of trans women should bear equal importance.

            1.2.4.2.1.5.3. Pro:
            There are few cases [352] of predators dressing up as women to access women-only spaces, despite changing rooms and many other women-only spaces being open to trans women for many years in most places.

        1.2.4.2.2. Con:
        Although women tend to be more cautious around strangers, most sexual assaults are carried out by men who are already close to the victim, such as intimate partners (p. 450 [353]).

          1.2.4.2.2.1. Con:
          Sexual assault is not the only security risk faced by women. Acts perpetrated by strangers, such as verbal assault, peeping, staring, and groping, also undermine women's sense of safety.

          1.2.4.2.2.2. Pro:
          Nearly all [354] sexual assaults reported by college women were perpetrated by someone known to the victim.

        1.2.4.2.3. Con:
        Predatory men can attack women in any number of places; there is no need to single out women-only spaces as different in this way.

          1.2.4.2.3.1. Pro:
          Inclusionary laws have not increased [355] the incidence of men entering women's spaces to attack them.

        1.2.4.2.4. Pro:
        Protesters [356] against the inclusion of trans women in Hampstead Heath Pond have argued that allowing anyone who claims to identify as a woman into their facilities would allow men with malicious intentions to access their space.

          1.2.4.2.4.1. Pro:
          Granting access to women-only spaces based solely on self-identification, as opposed to other forms of recognition, undermines essential sex-based protections [357].

          1.2.4.2.4.2. Con:
          Some of these protesters have been accused of transphobia [358]; their opinions should not necessarily be taken at face value.

            1.2.4.2.4.2.1. Con:
            Just because certain people within a protest have held views which are objectionable does not mean that the arguments that protesters are putting forward are illegitimate.

            1.2.4.2.4.2.2. Pro:
            The protesters referred to trans women as cross dressers [359]. This misgendering is undeniably transphobic.

      1.2.4.3. Con:
      Men would be deterred from falsely representing themselves to gain access to women-only spaces if society established a strong degree of trust between people allowed in those spaces and people who are clearly prohibited from entering.

        1.2.4.3.1. Pro:
        Although there is nothing besides well-established trust between men and women preventing them from doing so, the overwhelming majority of men avoid entering women-only spaces.

          1.2.4.3.1.1. Con:
          Since anyone AMAB represents a possible predator, women must rely on some men to protect women's spaces from other, potentially dangerous, men (pp. 10-11 [360]).

            1.2.4.3.1.1.1. Con:
            This argument is predicated on the belief that trans women are men rather than women (p. 10 [361]).

            1.2.4.3.1.1.2. Con:
            The idea that women must rely on men for protection is a form of benevolent sexism and reinforces patriarchal beliefs (p. 5 [362]).

            1.2.4.3.1.1.3. Pro:
            Some cis women find it difficult to develop a comparable level of trust [363] with trans people.

          1.2.4.3.1.2. Pro:
          As a result of internalized stigma, heterosexual men experience substantial discomfort when performing behaviors associated with femininity (pp. 136-137 [364]). Such discomfort discourages them from accessing women-only spaces.

        1.2.4.3.2. Con:
        Some men are inherently untrustworthy [365]; society needs rigid rules to protect the most vulnerable, especially women.

          1.2.4.3.2.1. Con:
          Some men are always going to be deceptive [366], but that should not make society distrust all men by default, as it would cause illegitimate harm to AMAB transgender people struggling to access women-only spaces.

        1.2.4.3.3. Pro:
        Trans women should value the norms and taboos that prevent men from entering women-only spaces and accept that there are valid reasons why they can and should be challenged [367] when entering such spaces.

        1.2.4.3.4. Con:
        A trust system would likely lead to conflict between trans women who decide to use women's spaces anyway and cis women who firmly oppose trans inclusion [368].

      1.2.4.4. Pro:
      Men's rights [369] groups have targeted women-only spaces in the past. If men are allowed to enter into such spaces by claiming to identify as women, these groups may use the opportunity to undermine and possibly destroy these spaces.

        1.2.4.4.1. Con:
        People who enter with problematic intent are likely to engage in equally problematic conduct, which would make it easy to remove them once they exhibit such behavior.

          1.2.4.4.1.1. Pro:
          Every space, whether public or private, sets up its own rules and guidelines. Anyone who breaks them can easily be asked to leave.

        1.2.4.4.2. Pro:
        Transphobic men's rights organizations [370] could take advantage of trans-inclusive laws to protest against trans rights.

          1.2.4.4.2.1. Pro:
          There are a number of men's rights groups that demonize [371] trans identity or describe it as an illness or conspiracy [372].

          1.2.4.4.2.2. Con:
          If women-only spaces expressly allow trans women, then anti-trans protesters in such spaces would be easier to remove, as the right of access would be formally established.

        1.2.4.4.3. Pro:
        Some men's rights groups are closely allied with trans advocacy [373] and actively opposed to feminism [374], as they believe that it promotes misandry [375]. Such groups might be interested in accessing feminist women-only spaces in order to subvert or destroy them.

          1.2.4.4.3.1. Con:
          Some men's rights groups that are supportive of trans people are also pro-feminist [376]. Such groups would be unlikely to cause problems in women-only spaces.

      1.2.4.5. Con:
      Trans women could be required to carry documentation that their biological sex does not correspond with their gender: a valuable deterrence for cis men, who would not be able to carry such paperwork.

        1.2.4.5.1. Con:
        To enforce this, cis women would be required to have medical evidence of their sex assigned at birth. This would be an added burden and privacy violation to cis women accessing these spaces.

        1.2.4.5.2. Pro:
        Many protected or exclusive spaces, such as hotels or government facilities, require proof of identity as a routine part of the entry process. Requiring documentation that certifies gender is no different in this regard.

          1.2.4.5.2.1. Con:
          Requiring proof of identity for entry would create issues for anyone carrying an "X" gender marker [377] on their ID.

          1.2.4.5.2.2. Con:
          Visits to hotels and government facilities are planned, while many visits to women-only spaces would be spontaneous or out of necessity; it is unlikely that people will have gender-identifying documents on their person at all times.

            1.2.4.5.2.2.1. Con:
            A simple ID card would not be overly burdensome to carry at all times; nearly everyone carries a wallet with their ID or driver's license.

          1.2.4.5.2.3. Pro:
          Carrying documentation is a small inconvenience in comparison with the problems that accompany being forced to use the facilities of one's assigned sex at birth.

            1.2.4.5.2.3.1. Con:
            While showing documentation is a minor inconvenience, the processes required to obtain such identification could still be a major obstacle [378].

            1.2.4.5.2.3.2. Pro:
            In 2019, 36% of the trans or nonbinary students who were prevented from using bathroom or locker facilities matching their identity reported being sexually assaulted [379].

        1.2.4.5.3. Pro:
        In England and Scotland, trans women can go through a legal process to change their registered sex on government-issued identification (p. 18 [380]).

          1.2.4.5.3.1. Con:
          This process does not necessarily prevent those with sinister motives from becoming certified. For example, a trans woman in the UK allegedly used transitioning as a deceptive tactic [381] to assault other inmates in a women-only prison block.

            1.2.4.5.3.1.1. Con:
            The miscategorization of a gender or social identity is not an acceptable reason to isolate [382] inmates from others; solitary confinement should be used strictly as punishment for breaking well-defined prison rules.

              1.2.4.5.3.1.1.1. Pro:
              Prisons that follow the Association for the Prevention of Torture (APT) guidelines, including most UN member states, treat inmates [383] in accordance with their gender.

            1.2.4.5.3.1.2. Pro:
            The accused would have been unlikely to gain access to an all-women prison block had it not been for favorable policies towards trans women.

            1.2.4.5.3.1.3. Pro:
            Someone using transitioning as a deceptive tactic proves that the fear over predatory men identifying as women to gain access to women-only spaces is not unfounded. Further development of the operational processes that allow trans women access to gendered spaces is needed.

            1.2.4.5.3.1.4. Con:
            An isolated extreme case involving a convicted criminal is not useful in assessing whether trans women should be allowed into women-only spaces.

              1.2.4.5.3.1.4.1. Con:
              This might not be an isolated case; there may be other similar cases that haven't gone public yet. The publicity from one case could help others find the courage or desire to report other incidents.

              1.2.4.5.3.1.4.2. Pro:
              The trans woman in question was transferred back to a men's prison [384] following the attack and is unlikely to ever be moved back to a women's prison.

          1.2.4.5.3.2. Pro:
          Allowing trans women to officially change their sex ensures that individuals who genuinely need women-only spaces can access them, while preventing individuals with sinister motives from gaining entry (pp. 4, 26, 42 [385]).

          1.2.4.5.3.3. Con:
          The process required to change such documentation is both lengthy and intrusive. An individual who wants to be medically certified as trans in the UK [386] is required to have lived as their identified gender for at least two years, undergo assessment by medical experts, and pay a £140 fee.

            1.2.4.5.3.3.1. Pro:
            Trans women undergoing such a certification process would still be required to use the men's facilities in the meantime. This puts them at unnecessary risk.

            1.2.4.5.3.3.2. Con:
            Given that trans identity is a central aspect of many trans people's lives, being medically certified would be worth the effort.

            1.2.4.5.3.3.3. Con:
            Lawmakers are considering amending the documentation change process by lowering the waiting time to three months, removing the required medical assessment, and lowering the fee to £5 (pp. 4, 20 [387]).

        1.2.4.5.4. Con:
        Requiring trans individuals to have medical certification before being permitted entry to women-only spaces is exclusionary.

          1.2.4.5.4.1. Con:
          Changing any aspect of one's identity, for example, nationality, is often a long process involving many procedures and verification checks. Changing one's gender is no different in this regard.

            1.2.4.5.4.1.1. Con:
            Changing gender on identification documents involves far less bureaucracy than being granted citizenship [388] in a new country.

            1.2.4.5.4.1.2. Pro:
            Even something seemingly simple, like a name change [389], can be a paperwork nightmare.

              1.2.4.5.4.1.2.1. Pro:
              This is especially true for young transgender individuals, who cannot legally change their names [390] without parental permission.

          1.2.4.5.4.2. Pro:
          The cost and time [391] required to obtain medical evidence is prohibitive for many individuals.

  1.3. Pro:
  Allowing anyone who identifies as a woman into women-only spaces would benefit both cis and trans women by improving their ability to work towards change in these spaces.

    1.3.1. Con:
    Women-only spaces have traditionally focused on issues that affect cis women, and many women today would prefer to honor this tradition.

      1.3.1.1. Con:
      There is evidence that trans women have existed throughout history [392] and have likely always been accepted [393] in women-only spaces, albeit under the radar [394] and not openly transgender.

        1.3.1.1.1. Pro:
        Priests of the Sumerian goddness Inanna, known as gala, were transgender (p. 23 [395]).

          1.3.1.1.1.1. Con:
          These priests lived separately from the rest of society, suggesting they were neither accepted nor welcomed like cis individuals.

        1.3.1.1.2. Pro:
        Many trans men have served the Christian church [396] throughout history, some of whom have been canonized as saints.

        1.3.1.1.3. Pro:
        Ancient Indian texts have acknowledged the existence of hijra, people who were AMAB but did not identify as men and typically chose to dress as women and adopt a collective dialect (p. 11 [397]).

        1.3.1.1.4. Con:
        With very few autobiographical accounts, clarifying the historical existence of trans women is difficult. If trans women were successful in maintaining stealth, then there would be no record of them as such (p. 4 [398]).

      1.3.1.2. Pro:
      Trans women should create their own spaces that would attend to their specific needs, rather than demand acceptance into exclusive spaces that were never intended to be all-inclusive.

        1.3.1.2.1. Con:
        Creating/maintaining a space exclusively for trans women is often impractical, as not every town or city has a sufficient number of trans women, making it difficult to organize and identify other individuals.

          1.3.1.2.1.1. Con:
          Although some places may not have a large enough population of trans women to support the creation of a designated space, trans women still have the option to travel to bigger cities or use online spaces for their community.

          1.3.1.2.1.2. Pro:
          If trans women are denied access to women-only spaces and must only use their own spaces, which may not exist due to demand, then many trans women will not have access to any such spaces.

          1.3.1.2.1.3. Pro:
          Not everyone has the means to travel to another city in order to find the care and support they require.

          1.3.1.2.1.4. Con:
          Many niche special interest groups [399] exist and are able to organize themselves with very few members; trans women would likely encounter few problems.

          1.3.1.2.1.5. Con:
          The struggle for trans women to create their own spaces does not mean that the gap should be bridged by women-only spaces.

            1.3.1.2.1.5.1. Pro:
            The burden of accommodating all oppressed groups [400] should not fall on women. Other spaces could take on the responsibility of being inclusive of trans women.

              1.3.1.2.1.5.1.1. Con:
              As trans women identify as women, not as members of other groups, it would not be appropriate or suitable for other spaces to take on the responsibility of including them.

            1.3.1.2.1.5.2. Con:
            Women-only spaces are the most logical space for trans women to access due to the inherent physical safety risks and discomfort [401] experienced in other types of spaces.

        1.3.1.2.2. Pro:
        Many women-only spaces have exclusionary membership policies [402] that limit access to some groups of women and prevent other women from gaining access.

          1.3.1.2.2.1. Pro:
          Many organizations collect dues or membership fees to help pay their bills and keep the cost of other services low, which could prevent some women from being able to access these spaces.

            1.3.1.2.2.1.1. Pro:
            Paying fees to join social clubs such as sororities and fraternities can deliver benefits that outweigh [403] the costs.

            1.3.1.2.2.1.2. Con:
            Exclusive membership policies based on one's financial ability [404] to pay membership fees reaffirm the economic divide in society, leading to deeper stratification.

              1.3.1.2.2.1.2.1. Con:
              Economic inequality is present in every aspect of life; there is no need to single out private members-only clubs.

              1.3.1.2.2.1.2.2. Con:
              Individual private clubs are unlikely to be wholly or partially responsible for deepening inequality in society at large.

              1.3.1.2.2.1.2.3. Pro:
              The government is complicit in deepening stratification by allowing private clubs to maintain "expressive association [405]" and exclude people based on social standing.

                1.3.1.2.2.1.2.3.1. Con:
                Any legislation that attempted to dictate how people choose to associate in private clubs would violate the First Amendment right to freedom of association [406].

            1.3.1.2.2.1.3. Con:
            Anyone identifying as a woman and accessing such spaces would still be expected to pay the same membership fees.

          1.3.1.2.2.2. Pro:
          Any private, non-tax-exempt club has the right to exclude people based on any number of otherwise protected categories (p. 235 [407]).

            1.3.1.2.2.2.1. Pro:
            Although gender is a protected category, and discrimination based on gender is illegal in public spaces and businesses, private clubs [408] remain able to choose members based on such criteria.

            1.3.1.2.2.2.2. Con:
            Under the 1965 US Civil Rights Act [409], such private clubs are narrowly defined, must truly reserve their facilities strictly for members, and have exclusive membership criteria.

          1.3.1.2.2.3. Pro:
          There are a number of sororities [410] that limit their membership to women from specific ethnic groups.

          1.3.1.2.2.4. Con:
          Policies that exclude specific groups of women are divisive and undermine the solidarity between women [411] that led to the creation of women-only spaces.

          1.3.1.2.2.5. Con:
          There are innumerable public and semi-public women-only spaces with no membership requirements, including bathrooms, fitting rooms, locker rooms, and lactation rooms.

        1.3.1.2.3. Con:
        Creating dedicated spaces for trans women and allowing trans women into existing women-only spaces are not mutually exclusive. Allowing trans women into women-only spaces would increase their available options.

        1.3.1.2.4. Pro:
        By design, exclusive spaces are created with a specific gender in mind. People who fall outside of the intended demographic are unlikely to benefit from inclusion.

          1.3.1.2.4.1. Pro:
          The staff at clinics and shelters may not have been sufficiently trained to medically or psychologically attend to trans women (p. 2 [412]). Thus, it may be better to create dedicated spaces that cater to the needs of trans women.

            1.3.1.2.4.1.1. Con:
            While many communities do not have the resources for more niche treatment and assistance, it is markedly better for trans women to have access to women's shelters that provide some form of accommodations and care than to go without any care at all.

              1.3.1.2.4.1.1.1. Pro:
              One in five [413] transgender individuals have experienced homelessness at some point in their lives. With so many homeless trans people, it is more important that they find some accommodation than receive niche assistance.

              1.3.1.2.4.1.1.2. Pro:
              Nearly 30% of homeless transgender individuals report being rejected from shelters due to their gender identity (p. 1 [414]). If an LGBTQ+ shelter is not available in their area, they are unlikely to be accepted anywhere.

              1.3.1.2.4.1.1.3. Con:
              If women's shelters are inexperienced with trans women, the minimal accommodations and care could end up being deadly, making trans women better off doing without [415].

            1.3.1.2.4.1.2. Pro:
            Many women-only spaces would be unwilling or unable to incur the cost of trans sensitivity training; without such training, staff may potentially insult or embarrass trans residents due to a lack of knowledge (p. 3 [416]).

              1.3.1.2.4.1.2.1. Con:
              Some organizations [417] offer trans sensitivity webinars free-of-charge or make training assistance available on a pay-what-you-can basis.

              1.3.1.2.4.1.2.2. Pro:
              Untrained staff may not understand the importance [418] of addressing a trans person by their preferred name and pronouns.

            1.3.1.2.4.1.3. Pro:
            Some shelters have rules regarding gender-appropriate dress codes [419]; if the staff are not respectful of a trans person's gender identity, this could be emotionally distressing.

              1.3.1.2.4.1.3.1. Con:
              Dress codes are inappropriate in domestic violence shelters, as controlling what the victim wears is common in cases of abuse and coercive control [420].

            1.3.1.2.4.1.4. Con:
            Some women's shelters have been offering support [421] to trans women for over a decade [422], and it seems as though both groups are willing to continue [423] and preserve that relationship.

          1.3.1.2.4.2. Pro:
          Women-only spaces can provide better healthcare services and facilities for cis women than for trans women (pp. 31, 34, 43 [424]).

            1.3.1.2.4.2.1. Pro:
            Women's health centers [425] utilize an integrated approach to treat diseases as a whole and focus specifically on how cis women experience diseases.

              1.3.1.2.4.2.1.1. Pro:
              Gender bias [426] in healthcare has led to inappropriate treatments for women, as healthcare professionals may treat women differently or at a lower quality than men with the same complaints.

              1.3.1.2.4.2.1.2. Con:
              Women's health centers cannot effectively address how women experience diseases, as the relevant literature [427] remains very scant and biased [428]; consequently, healthcare professionals do not possess the knowledge required for such attunement (pp. 7-8 [429]).

            1.3.1.2.4.2.2. Con:
            The care received by patients who visit women's health centers and general health clinics is not appreciably different (p. 68 [430]).

            1.3.1.2.4.2.3. Con:
            Government policies targeting trans women often cut funding [431] from clinics that provide services to all women.

            1.3.1.2.4.2.4. Pro:
            Women-only spaces that provide medical care may not have the facilities or experience to assist those who are undergoing or have undergone HRT (pp. 879, 880, 882 [432]).

          1.3.1.2.4.3. Con:
          Most LGBTQ+ organizations are not gender specific and are open to anyone who identifies [433] and wishes to join.

            1.3.1.2.4.3.1. Con:
            Some gender minority spaces are transphobic–like LGB without the T and the LGB alliance–and may not discuss things that affect trans women, such as violence against women and girls (VAWG [434]).

            1.3.1.2.4.3.2. Con:
            Misogyny within LGBTQ+ spaces is commonplace, especially among gold-star and platinum gays [435].

            1.3.1.2.4.3.3. Pro:
            Many cities have regularly scheduled events [436] for all members of the LGBTQ+ community.

        1.3.1.2.5. Pro:
        Trans women are isolated and alienated by a lack of access to spaces with the services and resources that they need (p. 11 [437]). If they had their own dedicated spaces, this would not be an issue.

          1.3.1.2.5.1. Pro:
          Within their own exclusive spaces, trans women would be able to discuss the issues they face without misunderstandings or judgments from cis people.

          1.3.1.2.5.2. Pro:
          The creation of women-only spaces is associated with increased agency and empowerment [438] for cis women; trans women will benefit similarly from having dedicated spaces.

          1.3.1.2.5.3. Pro:
          If there were accessible spaces that met all the needs of trans women, then it would not matter whether these spaces were inclusive or not.

          1.3.1.2.5.4. Con:
          Creating multiple, independent spaces is less cost effective [439] than consolidating groups under one space, which only requires resources for the minor modifications needed to accommodate one additional subgroup of women.

      1.3.1.3. Pro:
      Even if trans women were granted access to women-only spaces, there is no guarantee that cis women using such spaces would be open and accepting of them.

        1.3.1.3.1. Pro:
        In 2015, the women-only Michigan Womyn’s Music Festival [440] chose to shut down rather than accept the inclusion of trans women.

        1.3.1.3.2. Con:
        Even though some challenges have arisen when trans women access women-only spaces, staff members and other service users have been able to be inclusive of trans women and create a safe environment by being supportive and engaging (p. 13 [441]).

        1.3.1.3.3. Pro:
        Cis women may be unable to relate to the experiences and issues of trans women and prefer that their spaces be reserved for those with whom they share more in common.

          1.3.1.3.3.1. Pro:
          Many women-only spaces are focused on issues that are particular to a niche audience of cis women.

            1.3.1.3.3.1.1. Con:
            Although such spaces might have originally been intended for a niche audience of cis women, they can often benefit others outside of the original audience.

              1.3.1.3.3.1.1.1. Pro:
              Women-only addiction recovery groups are focused on an issue that affects both cis and trans women; spaces initially established to assist cis women with their recovery are equally relevant to trans women fighting addiction.

              1.3.1.3.3.1.1.2. Pro:
              Many issues, such as sexual assault [442], are not limited to cis women; such support groups should try to help anyone who is trying to recover from such experiences.

              1.3.1.3.3.1.1.3. Con:
              A group established with a niche set of interests should only grant access to those who share the group's interests; ancillary crossover is not a valid reason for granting access to others.

            1.3.1.3.3.1.2. Con:
            There are also many women-only spaces that are used for general purposes and not devoted to one particular issue.

            1.3.1.3.3.1.3. Pro:
            Women-only spaces organized around body type, religious affiliation, and/or ethnocultural background [443] provide women with a place to meet and interact with those who share a similar perspective and are focused on the same issues.

            1.3.1.3.3.1.4. Pro:
            Many women's groups are dedicated to a specific issue, such as support for domestic violence [444] victims or young mothers, and access is already restricted to cis women whose needs align with the focus of the group.

              1.3.1.3.3.1.4.1. Con:
              Simple linguistic measures can ensure that spaces are clearly reserved for people experiencing a particular issue. For example, trans women can also have children; using the phrasing "people with children" instead of "mothers" could help them feel more welcomed.

          1.3.1.3.3.2. Con:
          Cis women can find it difficult to relate to the experiences of other cis women as well.

            1.3.1.3.3.2.1. Pro:
            Lesbian women find it easier to relate to other lesbians than to heterosexual women (p. 473 [445]).

            1.3.1.3.3.2.2. Con:
            Consciousness-raising efforts have demonstrated that women-only spaces can simultaneously address the issues faced by different groups (pp. 242-243 [446]).

          1.3.1.3.3.3. Pro:
          Trans women also need and deserve spaces where others can relate to their experiences [447]; cis-inclusive spaces do not serve this need well.

      1.3.1.4. Con:
      Excluding trans women from women-only spaces because it has been that way for decades is an appeal to tradition [448]; just because something has historically been done one way does not mean that the practice should be continued.

        1.3.1.4.1. Pro:
        Many women-only cultural spaces were established by men in support of the patriarchy.

          1.3.1.4.1.1. Con:
          Turkish baths [449] function as an important gendered social space where women of all backgrounds can come together to relax and express themselves without the influence of men.

          1.3.1.4.1.2. Pro:
          Traditional home economics classes [450] in the 1950s were intended to prepare girls to become housewives and please their husbands.

          1.3.1.4.1.3. Con:
          Women have established [451] many women-only spaces, including several that were fundamental in establishing women's rights and beginning to dismantle the patriarchy.

          1.3.1.4.1.4. Pro:
          The first college to offer degrees to women, Georgia Female College (later changed to Wesleyan [452]), was founded by men and supported traditional gender norms (pp. 27-28 [453]).

        1.3.1.4.2. Pro:
        Some women-only spaces have been created and enforced by social or religious authority; in such cases, women may be eager to change the traditions.

          1.3.1.4.2.1. Pro:
          There is ongoing debate [454] within Islam about gender segregation in mosques; many Muslim women are challenging [455] this tradition, since women's spaces have traditionally been subpar [456] compared to men's spaces.

          1.3.1.4.2.2. Pro:
          Sex-segregated education [457] is associated with increased sexual discrimination and stereotyping; many women believe the practice to be unsound.

          1.3.1.4.2.3. Con:
          Many Orthodox Jewish women are fine with continuing the tradition of mechitzah [458] and would prefer that gendered spaces continue to be based on biological sex [459].

        1.3.1.4.3. Con:
        Women's spaces have traditionally been sanctuaries from male-dominated society [460]; opening them to anyone who identifies with womanhood is likely to lead to the erosion or outright abolition of these spaces.

          1.3.1.4.3.1. Con:
          Equating trans women with men based on internalized misogyny does not align with the desire to be rid of the patriarchy [461] and rigid gender roles.

          1.3.1.4.3.2. Pro:
          Many modern women-only spaces continue to provide this benefit [462] for their members and women who casually make use of these spaces.

        1.3.1.4.4. Con:
        Traditionally, birthing rooms are cisgender women-only spaces. The inclusion of transgender women may unintentionally evoke the presence of men and the harms associated with their presence.

          1.3.1.4.4.1. Pro:
          Some experts believe that childbirth [463] is safer in the absence of men. Women should be able to choose whether to allow AMAB people in such a space.

            1.3.1.4.4.1.1. Con:
            The opinions expressed in the source support misandrist [464] beliefs about childbirth and parenthood.

            1.3.1.4.4.1.2. Pro:
            There is historical precedent for this attitude; women were traditionally the caregivers and midwives [465].

            1.3.1.4.4.1.3. Con:
            As alluded to in the source, this may suggest that anxiety [466], rather than the presence of men, is the issue.

              1.3.1.4.4.1.3.1. Pro:
              When one gender enters spaces previously occupied solely by another gender, both groups will experience some level of anxiety until sharing the space becomes normalized. Anxiety, in itself, is not a valid reason to avoid sharing spaces.

          1.3.1.4.4.2. Con:
          The belief that the presence of men is a threat is bioessentialist [467] and perpetuates a reductive stereotype.

    1.3.2. Pro:
    The inclusion of trans women in women-only spaces sends the message that cis women perceive them as equals.

      1.3.2.1. Pro:
      Solidarity between cis and trans women helps both groups resist sexism and misogyny [468].

        1.3.2.1.1. Pro:
        Despite beliefs to the contrary, misogyny affects anyone who challenges its premises [469], regardless of their biological sex.

        1.3.2.1.2. Con:
        Some cis lesbians perceive trans activism as a form of misogyny [470] that is opposed to their recognition and rights.

      1.3.2.2. Pro:
      Efforts to portray trans and cis women as opposed undermine a long history of mutual support [471].

        1.3.2.2.1. Con:
        Some historical feminist groups have supported extremely hostile anti-trans perspectives [472].

        1.3.2.2.2. Pro:
        Many trans women have learned from, worked with, and been inspired by the work of cis feminists [473].

      1.3.2.3. Con:
      The last few decades have seen great strides towards equality for women [474] and gay people [475]. Trans women are attempting to take advantage of this push for equality [476] to co-opt the gains that cis women have made for their own benefit.

        1.3.2.3.1. Pro:
        Trans insistence on gender identity as distinct from biological sex is undermining the achievements of feminism [477].

        1.3.2.3.2. Con:
        Both cis and trans women can benefit from improved gender equality by supporting better education of boys and men [478] on gender issues, as harm against cis and trans women is more frequently perpetrated by men than women.

      1.3.2.4. Con:
      There are innumerable ways for cis women to demonstrate equality with trans women without including them in women-only spaces.

        1.3.2.4.1. Pro:
        Using the chosen names and preferred pronouns and honorifics [479] of trans people demonstrates respect for their identity and has positive effects on their mental health.

        1.3.2.4.2. Pro:
        Cis women can, and should, demonstrate equality by opposing proposed anti-trans legislation [480] seeking to limit the rights of trans people.

        1.3.2.4.3. Con:
        The most straightforward way to demonstrate equal standing with trans people is to accept them into the gendered spaces of their preferred identity.

    1.3.3. Pro:
    More inclusive women-only spaces are stronger [481] and help increase the representation of diverse voices.

      1.3.3.1. Pro:
      Some women-only spaces accept all individuals of marginalized genders [482] for a more inclusive and diverse representation of their mission against oppression.

        1.3.3.1.1. Pro:
        Those belonging to a marginalized group have better problem-solving skills [483] than non-marginalized people and homogeneous groups (p. 1 [484]).

          1.3.3.1.1.1. Con:
          Marginalization [485] is, by definition, dehumanizing. Any serendipitous positive outcomes of marginalization do not justify treating certain members of society as outcasts.

          1.3.3.1.1.2. Con:
          The positive effects of marginalization are reaped by members of the inner circle (p. 35 [486]) Thus, marginalized people pay a double price: the emotional toll of marginalization without any rewards for problem-solving contributions.

          1.3.3.1.1.3. Con:
          In the study referred to in the parent claim, marginalized people were included to solve non-marginalized problems (p. 14 [487]). The findings cannot be generalized to the real world, as marginalized people may not be offered the opportunity to solve such problems in the first place.

          1.3.3.1.1.4. Pro:
          The new perspectives offered by marginalized groups explain why diversity is associated with greater innovation [488].

          1.3.3.1.1.5. Pro:
          In general, distancing oneself from a problem [489] at hand is conducive to creativity, which facilitates problem-solving [490]. Marginalization may, thus, facilitate such a mechanism by inducing a sense of social distance or including a third party in the problem-solving process.

        1.3.3.1.2. Pro:
        Intersectional feminism [491], which includes trans women, is the best way to pursue equality [492], as it helps all women understand and recognize the intricacies of existing inequalities.

          1.3.3.1.2.1. Con:
          Some people view intersectional feminism as problematic because it prioritizes nonwhite, noncis women [493] by demonstrating how they have been oppressed throughout history.

            1.3.3.1.2.1.1. Con:
            Awareness of the historical oppression of some groups does not inherently grant special privileges to such groups, nor does it detract from awareness of the ways in which white cis women have been oppressed.

            1.3.3.1.2.1.2. Pro:
            Discussion of identity-based inequality can, at times, detract from discussion [494] about broader social issues in favor of personal experiences.

          1.3.3.1.2.2. Pro:
          Intersectional feminism is touted as the most complete mode of providing support and a voice for marginalized women, especially those identifying as trans [495].

        1.3.3.1.3. Pro:
        Some cis women with trans friends may feel conflicted and uncomfortable visiting women-only spaces which deny the gender identity of their friends.

        1.3.3.1.4. Con:
        Many women-only spaces, in particular bathrooms, are not accepting of marginalized genders; trans men have experienced hostilities [496] by cis women in women's bathrooms.

      1.3.3.2. Con:
      Women-only spaces, like all gendered spaces, perpetuate gender oppression.

        1.3.3.2.1. Pro:
        The existence of women-only spaces is founded upon the gender binary [497]. People should be able to decide for themselves which spaces meet their needs and interests regardless of their sex or gender.

          1.3.3.2.1.1. Con:
          Being predicated on the gender binary does not automatically imply gender oppression (pp. 17, 44 [498]).

          1.3.3.2.1.2. Pro:
          The choice to join a given space is often a matter of convenience; people are more inclined to join spaces which are closer to their home or place of employment.

          1.3.3.2.1.3. Pro:
          The gender binary is more accurately represented as a spectrum [499]; no one is purely masculine or feminine.

        1.3.3.2.2. Con:
        Gendered spaces can offer disadvantaged groups [500] a safe space to discuss their shared experiences, like motherhood in mother and baby groups.

        1.3.3.2.3. Con:
        Gendered spaces can also promote connectedness and empathy with a particular gender through consciousness raising [501] and mutual aid networks [502].

          1.3.3.2.3.1. Con:
          Under the guise of discussing gendered issues, some women-only spaces, such as finishing schools [503], reinforce gender stereotypes.

        1.3.3.2.4. Pro:
        Some courts have found that women-only gyms discriminate [504] unfairly against men.

          1.3.3.2.4.1. Con:
          One man sued [505] a women's gym for sex discrimination, but lost the case [506] because the complaint failed to meet the legal standard for discrimination and was judged to have been filed in bad faith.

          1.3.3.2.4.2. Con:
          Men are known to be particularly aggressive and unwelcoming to women [507] in mixed-gender gyms, suggesting that women-only gyms are likely necessary for women to feel comfortable working out.

            1.3.3.2.4.2.1. Pro:
            In the UK, single-sex organizations [508] are permitted under the Equality Act 2010, provided there is a good reason.

            1.3.3.2.4.2.2. Con:
            Sexual harassment occurs frequently in the workplace; although 38% of women [509] report having experienced sexual harassment at work, separating men's and women's workspaces would be considered discriminatory by most.

          1.3.3.2.4.3. Pro:
          The costs of litigation caused one women-only gym to close its doors [510] after a man felt discriminated against.

      1.3.3.3. Con:
      Since trans rights are framed as detrimental to women's rights [511] by some feminist groups, it is unlikely that the voices of trans women will be given equal prominence within women-only spaces.

        1.3.3.3.1. Con:
        The acceptance of trans voices in women-only spaces is a good place to start; equal prominence is a goal to be achieved at a later date.

          1.3.3.3.1.1. Pro:
          The infighting caused by the exclusion of trans women from women-only spaces weakens feminist groups in the long term.

        1.3.3.3.2. Con:
        The vast majority of feminist groups are not trans-exclusionary [512]; such groups pose little threat to the equal prominence of trans women's rights.

        1.3.3.3.3. Pro:
        Trans-exclusionary feminists believe that any move to separate womanhood from biological sex will undo difficult accomplishments, such as the creation of women-only spaces and services (p. 220 [513]).

          1.3.3.3.3.1. Pro:
          It has been argued that trans-inclusive policies will undermine sex-affirmative programs [514], such as Title IX, that are intended to address disparities faced by the female sex.

          1.3.3.3.3.2. Con:
          Women-only spaces and services will continue to exist regardless of who is granted access to them.

          1.3.3.3.3.3. Con:
          Trans-exclusionary feminists should be more concerned about the erosion of women-only spaces and services due to their alliance with right-wing men's hate groups [515].

        1.3.3.3.4. Pro:
        Spaces that include both trans women and trans-exclusionary feminists are unlikely to be productive [516] spaces for collaboration.

      1.3.3.4. Pro:
      Trans and cis women must share experiences in order to be exposed to and learn about the perspectives of other genders and create a more inclusive, empathetic, and cohesive community.

        1.3.3.4.1. Con:
        Trans and cis women can share experiences in other spaces while keeping women-only spaces free of people AMAB.

        1.3.3.4.2. Con:
        Not all women-only spaces encourage open discussion; for example, little to no discussion takes place in locker rooms and bathrooms, which limits their potential for building empathy and cooperation.

        1.3.3.4.3. Pro:
        Many cis women have learned valuable lessons about how gender and femininity operate within society [517] by interacting with trans women.

        1.3.3.4.4. Con:
        There are already plenty of mixed-gender spaces available for developing empathy and cooperation.

      1.3.3.5. Con:
      Greater diversity is not always a positive factor; there is less trust [518] between people in heterogeneous settings, which makes the group prone to infighting, increasing anxiety in gender-diverse spaces.

        1.3.3.5.1. Con:
        The amount of distrust in heterogeneous communities will decrease over time as people begin to trust one another [519].

        1.3.3.5.2. Con:
        Fomenting distrust only contributes to the development of echo chambers [520] between different groups of women regarding trans inclusion, furthering the divide between them.

        1.3.3.5.3. Pro:
        Gender-diverse groups have been found to experience less communication and greater levels of conflict (p. 58 [521]).

      1.3.3.6. Pro:
      Trans women have been and continue to be at the forefront of the struggle [522] for women's rights.

        1.3.3.6.1. Con:
        The women primarily responsible for the advancement of women's rights have been predominantly cis heterosexual [523].

          1.3.3.6.1.1. Con:
          Simple demographic differences [524] explain why the majority of women involved in the advancement of women's rights were cis heterosexual; there are far fewer trans than cis women.

            1.3.3.6.1.1.1. Pro:
            During the suffrage movement, there were likely even fewer trans women due to the near non-existence of LGTBQ+ rights, surgical methods [525], and hormonal therapies.

              1.3.3.6.1.1.1.1. Con:
              -> See 1.3.1.1.

              1.3.3.6.1.1.1.2. Pro:
              Evidence suggests that trans identity has become increasingly common in recent times; one study found that the number of trans teenagers has almost doubled since 2017 [526].

            1.3.3.6.1.1.2. Con:
            The existence of fewer people in absolute terms is irrelevant. Any dedicated trans women would have thrown themselves at the cause [527], resulting in a higher percentage of trans women involved in the advancement of women's rights.

          1.3.3.6.1.2. Con:
          Many of the women involved in the suffrage movement were bisexual, lesbian, or trans [528].

          1.3.3.6.1.3. Pro:
          Many famous women's rights activists have been cis hetero, including Bella Abzug [529], Malala Yousafzai [530], Kirsten Gillibrand [531], Sojourner Truth [532], Betty Friedan [533], and Coretta Scott King [534].

        1.3.3.6.2. Pro:
        Despite fighting for the rights of all women, trans women are frequently disenfranchised and left behind by legislation, forcing them to fight for their rights when cis men and women challenge their identities [535].

          1.3.3.6.2.1. Pro:
          The meteoric rise of legislation [536] regarding trans bathroom access, sports participation, and medical agency has brought the trans struggle to the forefront of public discourse.

          1.3.3.6.2.2. Pro:
          Trans women experience a number of issues when registering to vote and face significant legal and policy barriers to voting [537] in some US states.

          1.3.3.6.2.3. Pro:
          Mental health, one of the areas where trans women need the most assistance and where women-only mental health spaces could have a positive impact, is frequently overlooked [538] by lawmakers in the UK.

            1.3.3.6.2.3.1. Pro:
            Lawmakers in the UK have taken it a step further and banned conversion therapy [539] for gay and lesbian people, but not for trans people. This could prevent trans women from transitioning or having access to women-only spaces, and can have deleterious effects on their mental health.

          1.3.3.6.2.4. Con:
          Everyone who has been underserved or neglected by the law is disenfranchised and needs to fight for their rights; trans women are no different in this regard.

    1.3.4. Con:
    The inclusion of trans women in women-only spaces poses a linguistic challenge and limiting factor to women's progress in such spaces; women-only groups should not be forced to change their use of gendered language [540] in order to make trans women feel more welcome.

      1.3.4.1. Pro:
      Gendered language is normal to people who are raised under binary gender, and it is frequently used out of habit, rather than to consciously exclude anyone.

        1.3.4.1.1. Con:
        This line of thinking also excuses people who use racist or misogynistic language and claim they didn't "mean any harm by it."

      1.3.4.2. Pro:
      Trans women have been accused of seeking to regulate the language [541] used by cis women when speaking about gender issues. The expanded use of gender-neutral language may work against the interests of cis women by obscuring sex-based inequality.

        1.3.4.2.1. Con:
        The author has a personal bias [542] in her belief that transgender individuals shouldn't be allowed to self-identify as such, and that trans women should not have access to women-only spaces.

        1.3.4.2.2. Pro:
        While sex- and gender-based inequalities have some characteristics in common, they are distinct issues [543] and should be treated as such.

          1.3.4.2.2.1. Pro:
          Researchers have identified potential benefits of distinguishing between sex- and gender-related factors, as both can contribute to disparities in healthcare outcomes (pp. 12-13 [544]).

          1.3.4.2.2.2. Con:
          Gender inequality is based upon social attributes and roles that are attributed to the two biological sexes (pp. 946-947 [545]).

        1.3.4.2.3. Pro:
        There are a number of experiences particular to cis women [546] that are not shared by trans women. Changing the definition of the term "woman" in a way that does not include "female" obscures this distinction.

          1.3.4.2.3.1. Pro:
          Cis women are subject to a number of disparities in healthcare, such as an increased rate of breast cancer, that trans women do not experience (p. 3287 [547]).

            1.3.4.2.3.1.1. Pro:
            Groups specifically for survivors of ovarian or breast cancer should only be open to those who have had the same experience.

              1.3.4.2.3.1.1.1. Con:
              Trans women can and do develop breast cancer [548] and survivors or trans women fighting breast cancer would likely need access to women-only breast cancer support groups.

            1.3.4.2.3.1.2. Con:
            There are substantial health disparities between different groups of cis women as well; for example, Black women are significantly more likely to have lower cardiovascular health (CVH) than women of other ethnicities (p. 3 [549]). Such disparities are not a valid justification for exclusion.

          1.3.4.2.3.2. Con:
          There are equally as many, if not more, experiences particular to all women [550], whether cis or trans, that are distinct from those of men. Insisting on a definition of "woman" that requires "female" makes such distinctions more restrictive.

        1.3.4.2.4. Pro:
        There has been a recent shift [551] away from describing trans women as "identifying as women" in favor of simply referring to them as women, which does not account for biological sex.

          1.3.4.2.4.1. Pro:
          Gender-critical feminists [552] purposefully use exclusionary language in order to perpetuate the notion that being female is an essential aspect of womanhood.

        1.3.4.2.5. Con:
        Denying the need for inclusive language definitively silences groups who are entirely shut out of conversations, as they have been for centuries.

      1.3.4.3. Con:
      Socially and linguistically, trans people face erasure through medical and legal discrimination (p. 358 [553]). It is unethical for women-only groups to contribute to such discrimination.

        1.3.4.3.1. Pro:
        Erasure exacerbates stress and negatively impacts the mental health of trans individuals as it makes it difficult for them to further develop their identity (p. 38 [554]).

        1.3.4.3.2. Pro:
        Given that widespread discussion of gender identity and the shift towards choosing personal pronouns [555] have only occurred more recently [556], these terms could take some time to make their way into the medical and legal fields.

        1.3.4.3.3. Con:
        There is a modern trend toward more inclusive legislation [557], representation, and policies.

        1.3.4.3.4. Con:
        Modernizing the terminology used in the medical and legal fields is outside of the purview of most women-only groups; the individuals responsible for the administration and operations of these industries should fix these issues.

      1.3.4.4. Con:
      Phrases such as "pussy power [558]" are problematic because they imply that a woman needs to have a vagina [559] in order to be an authentic woman. The presence of trans women in women-only spaces will help educate cis women that such language is not inclusive.

        1.3.4.4.1. Con:
        The phrase "pussy power" is not entirely anti-trans, since some trans women have undergone gender-affirming surgery and have surgically constructed vaginas [560].

          1.3.4.4.1.1. Con:
          Not all trans women have the desire or financial capacity [561] to undergo gender-affirming surgery.

        1.3.4.4.2. Pro:
        The disagreement of trans women with phrases such as "pussy power" has led to some all-women spaces being cancelled [562].

        1.3.4.4.3. Con:
        Some recent protests using the "pussy power" slogan have been responses to misogynistic statements about women [563] from former US President Donald Trump. The phrase was used as a way of reclaiming the term and was not intended to exclude trans people.

        1.3.4.4.4. Pro:
        Other similar phrases, such as "no uterus, no opinion," also exclude trans women and are rooted in the misogynistic belief that women are defined by their genitalia [564].

      1.3.4.5. Pro:
      For centuries, women's identities have been erased by androcentric language (e.g., equating "man" with "human"). The use of terms referencing female genitalia is an attempt to assert female identity.

        1.3.4.5.1. Pro:
        Terms describing female genitalia have typically evoked feelings of shame or disgust. Feminists, such as Eve Ensler, who wrote The Vagina Monologues [565], use these terms to normalize their usage in everyday language.

        1.3.4.5.2. Pro:
        Many gendered languages default [566] to giving the masculine form power over the feminine form. This excludes women and people who don't fall within the binary.

        1.3.4.5.3. Con:
        The assertion of female identity should be inclusive and not use language that forcibly oppresses others who also identify as women.

      1.3.4.6. Con:
      Some medical support and rape crisis centers are already evolving towards the use of more inclusive terminology [567], thus decreasing discrimination.

  1.4. Pro:
  Women-only spaces should strive to be as inclusive as possible to ensure that everyone is treated equally.

    1.4.1. Con:
    Such decisions should be made solely by the individual business owner [568].

      1.4.1.1. Con:
      In instances where the private sector is the de facto provider of women-only spaces, the state should regulate them in order to ensure that women have meaningful access to the necessary facilities.

        1.4.1.1.1. Pro:
        Vital state-funded services, such as women's shelters, are often subject to budget cuts [569] during periods of economic recession. Under these circumstances, privately-run charities often become the primary source of of women-only spaces.

        1.4.1.1.2. Con:
        The US state of Tennessee recently passed legislation requiring businesses that base access to gendered spaces on gender identity to post red and yellow warning signs [570] to that effect. Such regulations can easily result in discrimination against both individuals and the businesses themselves.

      1.4.1.2. Con:
      Allowing private business owners to determine who is considered a woman may lead to gender-based discrimination.

        1.4.1.2.1. Pro:
        Other forms of discrimination, such as race-based discrimination, are already deemed unacceptable and, in most cases, illegal [571]. Considering trans status as a legitimate reason for discrimination is therefore hypocritical.

        1.4.1.2.2. Con:
        Policies applied to private groups and spaces are unlikely to affect national laws or wider social beliefs about gender.

        1.4.1.2.3. Con:
        Many businesses would likely establish rules without strictly enforcing them [572], opting to let common sense prevail.

          1.4.1.2.3.1. Pro:
          Many gendered spaces, such as gender-segregated bathrooms and locker rooms, already exist based on common sense with little enforcement necessary.

        1.4.1.2.4. Pro:
        Women-only gyms have a history of being discriminatory [573] towards trans women.

      1.4.1.3. Pro:
      As private businesses are often considered liable for things that occur in their bathrooms and must pay insurance to cover such liabilities, they should be empowered to make determinations about the use of their bathrooms.

        1.4.1.3.1. Con:
        Businesses that fail to adhere to civil rights legislation [574] regarding gender discrimination may also be held liable.

        1.4.1.3.2. Pro:
        A business can be held liable for negligent security [575] in cases of a third-party assault that occurs on their property.

      1.4.1.4. Pro:
      In some areas where people are required to use facilities based on their sex at birth, private businesses can decide [576] to allow access based on gender identity. This sets a standard based on sex, but empowers business owners to set different rules based on their values.

    1.4.2. Pro:
    Not including trans women in women-only spaces would set a bad precedent for how people should be treated in gendered spaces.

      1.4.2.1. Con:
      Given that women-only and men-only spaces are wholly separate, it is unlikely that the rules of one will determine the rules of the other.

        1.4.2.1.1. Con:
        The rules that govern women- and men-only spaces, such as Title IX [577], require that men's and women's programs receive equitable participation opportunities, scholarships, and facilities, even if they are not identical.

        1.4.2.1.2. Pro:
        Men-only spaces have not been subjected to the same scrutiny and moral panic [578] as women-only spaces.

      1.4.2.2. Pro:
      Requiring transgender people to use spaces that match the sex they were assigned at birth would require transgender men to use women-only spaces, placing themselves and others at risk of distress.

        1.4.2.2.1. Pro:
        Refusing to accept the gender identity of trans men is a violation of their basic human rights [579].

        1.4.2.2.2. Pro:
        The presence of trans men in a women-only space could have a negative impact on the women using that space.

          1.4.2.2.2.1. Con:
          Having trans men in a women-only space might be acceptable if a particular women-only space is explicit [580] in supporting anyone who isn't a cisgender man.

          1.4.2.2.2.2. Pro:
          Trans men are granted the privileges [581] associated with being a man once they transition. They would not be able to relate to the patriarchal oppression that women still go through.

            1.4.2.2.2.2.1. Con:
            Trans men are likely to have experienced patriarchal oppression [582] before and during their transition.

            1.4.2.2.2.2.2. Pro:
            The salaries of trans men increase slightly upon transition, while the salaries of trans women fall by one-third [583].

        1.4.2.2.3. Con:
        Due to safety concerns, many trans men already avoid men-only spaces, particularly changing rooms, as they risk verbal and physical attacks [584].

      1.4.2.3. Pro:
      If trans women are not included in women-only spaces, then they will be required to use facilities based on their sex assigned at birth.

        1.4.2.3.1. Pro:
        Restricting bathroom access for transgender individuals is detrimental [585] to their physical and mental health, as it places them at risk and can lead to them avoiding public bathrooms entirely.

          1.4.2.3.1.1. Pro:
          Fear of being verbally or physically assaulted for using what others view as the wrong bathroom [586] can cause trans people to avoid using the bathroom altogether, which is bad for their health.

          1.4.2.3.1.2. Pro:
          Preventing transgender children from using facilities that match their gender identities makes them targets of additional bullying and abuse [587] and increases their risk of being sexually assaulted [588].

          1.4.2.3.1.3. Con:
          Transgender individuals are not being prohibited from going to the bathroom; they still have bathrooms they can use.

        1.4.2.3.2. Con:
        Some nonconforming trans women may already use facilities based on their sex assigned at birth and be fine with continuing to do so.

          1.4.2.3.2.1. Pro:
          Gender identity and expression (or presentation) are distinct from each other [589]; one can identify as a trans woman and choose not to conform to the expectations of their gender.

      1.4.2.4. Con:
      Bathroom bills like HB2 [590] in North Carolina, and a similar proposal in Texas [591], were introduced in 2017 to prevent sexual predators from accessing women-only spaces by claiming to identify as trans women.

        1.4.2.4.1. Con:
        The approval of HB2 sparked massive political and economic backlash [592], and it was ultimately repealed.

          1.4.2.4.1.1. Con:
          Rather than pushing for an immediate repeal of HB2, a pause and reconsideration of the legislation [593] would have given trans and gender-nonconforming advocates more time to organize and work on stronger anti-discrimination legislation.

            1.4.2.4.1.1.1. Pro:
            The repeal of HB2 led to the passage of HB 142 [594], a measure with less clear guidance that has resulted in an escalation of hateful behavior [595].

          1.4.2.4.1.2. Pro:
          There is increasing public outcry [596] against places that institute anti-LGBTQ+ measures.

        1.4.2.4.2. Pro:
        Many women-only spaces are not private or secured and only rely on tradition and goodwill to keep women safe.

          1.4.2.4.2.1. Pro:
          Basing access to women-only spaces on biological sex is one of the traditions that keep these spaces safe (pp. 6, 18 [597]).

          1.4.2.4.2.2. Con:
          Anti-trans conservatives have often used the appeal to tradition as justification for denying the rights of trans people; similar tactics [598] were used by those who were opposed to racial integration.

            1.4.2.4.2.2.1. Pro:
            Opponents of trans-inclusive bathroom access policies have used unethical rhetoric and fear tactics to create panic and demonize trans people [599].

        1.4.2.4.3. Con:
        HB2 and the similar proposal in Texas were created in bad faith as attempts to prevent transgender people [600] from using the bathrooms that correspond to their gender identity.

          1.4.2.4.3.1. Pro:
          HB2 also included a repeal of any local nondiscrimination legislation at odds with North Carolina state law; this has enabled legal discrimination against individuals based on gender or sexual orientation [601].

      1.4.2.5. Pro:
      Excluding trans women from women-only spaces can lead to or perpetuate transphobia [602].

        1.4.2.5.1. Pro:
        Refusing to acknowledge trans women as women and seeking to deny them access to bathrooms [603] is often motivated by transphobia.

          1.4.2.5.1.1. Pro:
          Accepting someone's claimed discomfort with those who don't easily fit into their own predetermined categories enables transphobia [604].

            1.4.2.5.1.1.1. Pro:
            Misogynists and transphobic people are not interested in how trans people identify, but how they appear [605].

          1.4.2.5.1.2. Con:
          Women with a strong stance against the inclusion of trans women in women-only spaces may keep their opinions to themselves due to the fear of being cancelled [606] or labeled transphobic.

        1.4.2.5.2. Con:
        Transphobia is often overstated in women-only spaces. Weaponized allegations of oppression [607] lead to the intimidation and censorship [608] of gender-critical dissenters.

          1.4.2.5.2.1. Pro:
          Labels like "transphobic" and "alt-right" have a high emotional impact and can be misused to discredit someone without actually addressing their arguments [609].

          1.4.2.5.2.2. Pro:
          Trans activists have physically blocked room access [610] and actively harassed event organizers [611] with emails, tweets, and/or phone calls in attempts to prevent talks or speeches where speakers question the efforts of trans advocacy groups.

          1.4.2.5.2.3. Pro:
          Despite having campaigned for more than 50 years for equal rights, gay rights activist Peter Tatchell was nonetheless labeled racist and transphobic and, as a result, had to confront no-platforming [612] in 2016.

            1.4.2.5.2.3.1. Con:
            Being an advocate for one marginalized group does not mean one is supportive of all marginalized groups.

          1.4.2.5.2.4. Con:
          The intolerance of dissent [613] is a widespread problem in modern society and is not unique to trans activist groups.

          1.4.2.5.2.5. Con:
          Transgender activists have publicly expressed their regrets over having used tactics [614] that silence others, particularly other cis women.

          1.4.2.5.2.6. Pro:
          Trans activists actively create a climate [615] in which questions and criticisms of their ideas are not only discouraged, but sometimes outright punished.

          1.4.2.5.2.7. Con:
          Similar logic has been used to discredit Black activist groups highlighting racism in society by claiming they are silencing criticism (pp. 2-3 [616]).

        1.4.2.5.3. Pro:
        Most cis women actively support [617] the inclusion of trans women in women-only spaces. The opinions of a transphobic minority should not be taken as representative.

          1.4.2.5.3.1. Pro:
          Women have been challenging the exclusion of women from men-only spaces steeped in tradition (p. 14 [618]). It would be hypocritical of women to exclude other subgroups of women in the name of tradition.

          1.4.2.5.3.2. Con:
          Opposing trans women [619] entering women-only spaces does not imply that someone is transphobic [620]. They may have reasons to think this apart from aversion or disdain for trans people.

            1.4.2.5.3.2.1. Con:
            Statements referring to a trans woman as dangerous to children, "a man who says he's a woman," and "a person with a penis and a beard coming to the pool and getting his kit off," can easily be construed as transphobic [621].

            1.4.2.5.3.2.2. Pro:
            Some women might be opposed to the personal inconvenience caused by invasive or lengthy tests, rather than to the presence of trans women themselves.

          1.4.2.5.3.3. Con:
          Twitter hashtags, even trending ones, are not representative of the population overall, as they can be accessed or manipulated by anyone, not just the population to which they pertain.

        1.4.2.5.4. Pro:
        Denying trans women access to basic services contributes to the discursive treatment of trans people as abnormal and legitimizes discrimination against them [622].

      1.4.2.6. Con:
      The inclusion of trans women in women-only spaces is a distinct issue and should be considered on its own merits, not on its relationship to other situations.

        1.4.2.6.1. Pro:
        There are concerns specific to the intersection of cis and trans women that do not apply in other gendered spaces (pp. 177-178 [623]).

        1.4.2.6.2. Con:
        Gender issues are essentially human rights issues (p. 2 [624]); access to women-only spaces should be governed by the same principles applied in all gendered spaces.

      1.4.2.7. Pro:
      -> See 1.1.3.1.2.1.

    1.4.3. Con:
    In order to improve equality, women-only spaces should be organized by sex [625], not gender, as discrimination is generally based on sex.

      1.4.3.1. Pro:
      -> See 1.1.2.1.3.

      1.4.3.2. Pro:
      Women-only athletic competitions organized by sex have been marred by the protests of women over the inclusion of non-AFAB athletes.

        1.4.3.2.1. Pro:
        Cisgender high school girls in Connecticut, USA, have found it unfair [626] that trans women athletes are allowed to compete in races against cis women athletes. They filed a lawsuit, claiming that this violated their right to a fair competition.

          1.4.3.2.1.1. Con:
          Days after the lawsuit was filed, one of the cis girl athletes defeated one of the transgender athletes [627] in a Connecticut state championship, demonstrating that everyone benefits from the increased level of competition when trans girls are allowed to compete on girls teams.

        1.4.3.2.2. Pro:
        A group of University of Pennsylvania swimmers requested the removal [628] of a trans teammate, claiming that her biological sex gave her an unfair advantage over the competition.

        1.4.3.2.3. Con:
        Trans women have used the controversy surrounding trans inclusion on sports teams to highlight the inequalities that are faced by all women in sports (p. 2 [629]).

          1.4.3.2.3.1. Pro:
          Athletes have observed how the widespread controversy over trans inclusion in women's sports is contrasted by minimal media coverage [630] of unequal facilities and funding, demonstrating a lack of concern about equity for women.

          1.4.3.2.3.2. Con:
          If trans women were truly concerned about the rights of all women, they would not seek to utilize the limited funds and facilities [631] that have been earmarked for women's athletics.

          1.4.3.2.3.3. Pro:
          Transgender athletes are drawing attention [632] to the sex/gender entanglement in sports that is paralleled and increasingly problematized by society at large.

        1.4.3.2.4. Con:
        Many of these cases are isolated, insignificant examples [633] that have been overscrutinized by the media and portrayed as more pressing issues than they actually are.

        1.4.3.2.5. Con:
        There is very little appreciable difference [634] in the athletic performance of cis women and trans women undergoing hormonal therapy.

          1.4.3.2.5.1. Pro:
          Testosterone suppressants reduce hemoglobin levels [635] in trans women to the same level as cis women, eliminating an innate advantage enjoyed by cis men over cis women in athletics.

          1.4.3.2.5.2. Con:
          Some trans women still enjoy natural physical advantages over cis women, including bone and muscle structure (p. 199 [636]).

      1.4.3.3. Con:
      Women-only spaces are defined by gender and not sex. Denying trans women entry into women-only spaces is discriminatory and furthers the incorrect belief that biological sex determines an individual's gender.

        1.4.3.3.1. Pro:
        The brain is largely responsible [637] for how one experiences gender.

        1.4.3.3.2. Con:
        Biological theorists posit that biological sex does determine an individual's gender [638].

        1.4.3.3.3. Pro:
        The exclusion of trans women from women-only spaces creates an unfair prejudice, as trans men and AFAB nonbinary people, who do not identify as women, are still allowed into such spaces.

      1.4.3.4. Con:
      Although spaces organized by sex can generally address biological needs (p. 228 [639]), they do not adequately account for social factors [640]. Spaces organized by gender can include such considerations, and are therefore superior.

        1.4.3.4.1. Pro:
        Gender and sex shape and influence each other [641]; consideration of the interactions between the two is essential to design effective spaces.

        1.4.3.4.2. Con:
        Women-only spaces organized by sex have historically addressed numerous social issues [642].

    1.4.4. Pro:
    Trans women and cis women are both subgroups of women and have equal rights to claim their gender and access women-only spaces (p. 3 [643]).

      1.4.4.1. Con:
      Gender-critical feminists [644] strongly disagree with the notion that trans women are a subgroup of women. Thus, they may perceive the exclusion of trans women as beneficial to cis women.

        1.4.4.1.1. Con:
        -> See 1.4.2.5.1.

        1.4.4.1.2. Pro:
        Granting trans women access to women-only spaces inherently undermines [645] the realization of cis women's rights.

      1.4.4.2. Con:
      Not all societies afford legitimacy to trans identity [646]. According to the model of gender in such societies, trans women are men.

        1.4.4.2.1. Con:
        Gender identity is an internal personal experience [647], and thus is not dependent upon the beliefs of others.

          1.4.4.2.1.1. Pro:
          Gender identity [648] is a personal sense of one's own gender, so if somebody identifies as a woman, they are one.

            1.4.4.2.1.1.1. Pro:
            Some trans women have always felt [649] like women; being AMAB is beyond their control.

              1.4.4.2.1.1.1.1. Pro:
              In a cisnormative society, it is often assumed [650] that people's gender will match the sex they are assigned at birth.

              1.4.4.2.1.1.1.2. Con:
              Since gender identity is socially informed, most people, whether trans or cis, do not have a well-developed gender identity [651] prior to adolescence.

            1.4.4.2.1.1.2. Con:
            Unlike other prefixes, "trans" connotes a pre-post difference in identity that requires "woman" to be redefined by erasing the early biological and sociological realities of cis women.

              1.4.4.2.1.1.2.1. Pro:
              Trans people are often just called "trans" for short [652], but this is not the case for cis women; thus, being trans is a defining aspect of the trans identity, which is not shared by cis women. This demarcates the boundaries between the two groups of women.

                1.4.4.2.1.1.2.1.1. Con:
                The reason why trans people are often referred to as trans, whereas cis people are not called cis, is a cisnormative [653] assumption based on the disparity [654] in population sizes.

                1.4.4.2.1.1.2.1.2. Con:
                Trans women need not be referred to as "trans," they can also be simply referred to as women.

                1.4.4.2.1.1.2.1.3. Pro:
                Due to the variety of terms [655] that can be used to refer to trans individuals, it can be difficult to differentiate and determine the best option without asking first. This is not the case for cis women who are simply referred to as women.

                  1.4.4.2.1.1.2.1.3.1. Con:
                  A wide variety of terms [656] can also refer to cis women, including cishet [657], demigirl [658], apathetic [659], and nonconforming [660].

              1.4.4.2.1.1.2.2. Con:
              The term "transgender [661]" does not imply anything about the prior state of a person's identity; it refers to a discrepancy between a person's identity and their sex assigned at birth.

            1.4.4.2.1.1.3. Con:
            Regardless of how one identifies or expresses their gender, others may not perceive them the same way.

              1.4.4.2.1.1.3.1. Pro:
              A person that appears male entering a women-only space may still be penalized or denied access in many places, regardless of their gender identity.

          1.4.4.2.1.2. Con:
          Subjective personal experiences do not necessarily conform with objective truth. Making demands on society [662] based solely on personal experience is unreasonable.

        1.4.4.2.2. Pro:
        In order to stay aligned with the norms of such cultures, trans women should be excluded from women-only spaces.

          1.4.4.2.2.1. Pro:
          Gender norms are important to society (pp. 4-5 [663]); the small population [664] of trans women does not justify changing deeply held values.

            1.4.4.2.2.1.1. Con:
            Supporting norms that delegitimize [665] the identities of trans people is unethical.

          1.4.4.2.2.2. Con:
          This is an example of status quo bias [666]. The issues that affect women have changed over time [667], and continuing some traditions may not be justifiable.

            1.4.4.2.2.2.1. Pro:
            Spaces and social norms typically evolve when there is an increase in the understanding of something (p. 936 [668]). Preserving tradition for its own sake merely preserves outdated information.

              1.4.4.2.2.2.1.1. Pro:
              Traditionally, it was assumed that sex and gender were the same thing. However, being a woman is now understood as distinct [669] from being female, so women-only spaces should adapt accordingly.

            1.4.4.2.2.2.2. Con:
            Many of the serious issues faced by women [670] around the world remain unchanged.

        1.4.4.2.3. Pro:
        In some societies, certain gender and sexual identities constitute criminal offenses [671].

        1.4.4.2.4. Con:
        A number of people believing in a model does not make it true. Despite being incorrect, the geocentric model [672] was widely accepted for many centuries.

      1.4.4.3. Pro:
      Access to spaces which coincide with one's gender identity bolsters one's personal identity, which is an essential human right per the Universal Declaration of Human Rights (pp. 5-6 [673]).

        1.4.4.3.1. Pro:
        When the right to an identity is not supported, trans people—especially children—can experience bigotry and prejudice [674].

        1.4.4.3.2. Con:
        The right to develop a personal identity is not upheld by every country (pp. 2-4 [675]).

      1.4.4.4. Pro:
      Womanhood [676] is not defined by the presence of certain biological features, such as a uterus or breasts.

        1.4.4.4.1. Pro:
        The belief that women are defined by anatomy [677] has been used to stigmatize groups of women and ignore the experiences of countless women who vary from the norm.

        1.4.4.4.2. Con:
        Women who have undergone a hysterectomy are still considered women because they previously had a uterus.

    1.4.5. Pro:
    Some nonbinary people also have a legitimate need to access [678] women-only spaces.

      1.4.5.1. Pro:
      Some nonbinary people menstruate [679] and thus reasonably require access to women-only services and resources that support this experience.

        1.4.5.1.1. Pro:
        Period stigma [680] is more damaging to trans men and nonbinary people who experience periods than to cis women.

      1.4.5.2. Pro:
      Some femme nonbinary people have a feminine appearance and are thus subject to a level of sexual harassment comparable to women; as such, they may benefit from access to women-only safe spaces [681].

      1.4.5.3. Con:
      Non-binary people are often associated with womanhood, as a form of woman-lite [682], despite not identifying as such; their inclusion in woman-only spaces continues this disrespectful treatment.

        1.4.5.3.1. Con:
        Permission to access women-only spaces is not the same as an obligation to use them; nonbinary people are fully capable of choosing for themselves whether or not they wish to enter such spaces based on their personal needs.

        1.4.5.3.2. Pro:
